Smiled the \emph{door shut} while Sella Vail’s hand hovered. Took Mira’s crust of Vail-smoked barley bread.

\item Ch 2: On Day 2 coughed from the pits, called it weather, rubbed the pale sieve-wire scar and got specific about steps, loaves and the day of the month; said a body can be warm and still be gone without looking at the child. On the third morning got up while Sella was at the bench, followed the covered bundle to the end of the Clay Yard and took none of the nineteen steps after it. Pulse in throat, thumb on scar, the door-shut smile; said nothing and did not say his name. Heard Alda say he had gone in the levy among the children taken for the account, asked only \emph{where}, and asked nothing further as the dates went on arriving. She believes him spent and will believe it three years.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Day 6, dusk. Stood at the threshold of House Silt three doors down with her shoulder against the jamb as Sella carried the basket past. The cough came up out of the pits and she said \emph{weather}; her thumb went to the pale sieve-wire scar, the door-shut smile came down, and the nineteen steps went out of her feet and into her thumb, counted because the count kept a larger thing out of the room. She did not look into the basket. The boy’s mouth worked under the wool and Sella shifted the basket up against her hip and went on toward the bed. She does not know her son is in it and will not say the number or the name.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Day 9, morning, at the edge of the fish-trade in the north bridge’s shade. Stood at the end of nineteen steps with her shoulder against nothing, slip-grey nails, wet clay dried at her hem and the pale sieve-wire scar up the inside of her left forearm. Coughed from the pits in a low short way and called it weather, then put the door-shut smile back on her face. Said \emph{nineteen out and nineteen back}, four loaves on the seventh of Stone in eight hundred and twelve, three to the levy and one eaten with her feet up, and bits to the woman who comes with a basket; smoothed the scar with her thumb. Counted the nineteenth into her thumb again and did not look into the basket, which held her son, and did not say the number or the name.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Garl Vail}{the man whose warmth was left where it lay}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Garl, Garl of the Vail, the dead wheel-keeper, the uncollected}\CRfield{Role}{family}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 775, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; fourth generation of House Vail}\CRfield{Allegiance}{House Vail kiln-house; Kiln-Masters of Sill}\CRfield{Status}{dead (Stone, Y.H. 811; uncollected in the Long Cold; warmth lost to the Waste)}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 2}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
He is known in the house by objects rather than by memory. The wheel in the shed has a seat worn hollow on the left side where he sat for twenty years, and Sella will not let anyone fill it. A coat of his hangs on a peg behind the door with the salt stains still in it, and she has never taken it down, though Jorra has twice reached for it in the dark and stopped. He was a big man with clay in the creases of his hands and a laugh that ended in a cough; his left thumb was shaped by the wheel into a permanent hook. People who saw him said he moved slowly and arrived early, which in a kiln-house is the same thing. He smelled of wet clay and banked coals. In the house the absent smell is stronger than the memory: when the kiln is cold and the Ash ridge is thick, the room smells the way the corner of a room smells where something was left too long.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
He is remembered in proverbs, which means his voice has been improved by use and is probably not as good as the versions of it that survive. He never said a hard word in front of the fire — a habit Jorra has inherited without knowing it — and when he was angry he went to the wheel. His sayings are all about repair and none of them are about completion.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “If it’s worth mending it’s worth mending twice. The second time is the real one.”

\item “Don’t tell me the pot’s ruined. Tell me what it was before it was ruined.”

\item “Feed the fire before you feed yourself. It’ll still be there in the morning. You won’t.”

\item “A man’s hands know what his head’s still arguing about.”

\item “Leave the crack showing. People trust a thing they can see has been handled.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
Fourth generation of Vails, born in the kiln-house and married to Sella, a slip-mixer’s daughter, in Y.H. 796. He kept the wheel for twenty years, threw the tiles that went into the floor of the Cross market, and was offered a price for his measure by a Sere factor in Y.H. 809 which he refused and did not mention for a year. He had two children: Jorra, born Y.H. 796, and Enna, born Y.H. 802, who died at nine months of the second chill and whose death he sat through without being able to do anything that mattered. In Y.H. 810 he began to be what Low Kiln calls walking thin — a vessel leaking faster than a man of his trade should leak — and he said nothing about it, because a kiln-master who admits a flaw loses the wheel.

In the Long Cold of Y.H. 811 the Ilm lay under ice for ninety-four days and no collector could cross from Greybank. Garl’s vessel gave in the first weeks of Stone. He died at home with Sella beside him and no iron tube in the room, and his warmth went where warmth goes when nobody takes it: into the Waste, unbanked, unrecorded, unreturned. In the morning there was grey Ash along the skirting board thicker than anyone had seen in that house. Tarn was sent to find a collector and turned back at the frozen ford. Jorra was thirteen and took the wheel a week later. The Vail household has been cool ever since, in a way that has nothing to do with the weather.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He knew he was leaking and kept throwing. That is the wound and it is also the answer to any question about why he is dead. He feared being banked thin and coming back as someone who would stand in his own house and not know the wheel; he feared more than that being spent whole and returned beautiful for somebody else’s benefit, which is what he had seen happen to a man he would not name. His contradiction: a man who mended pots for a living would not let anyone mend him, and called the refusal \emph{not wanting to be a bother}, which is the gentlest lie in the district and the most expensive.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wanted to leave the wheel to Jorra whole, with the seat still worn and the note still true, and to come back as himself one day and teach the boy what a full note feels like under the knuckle. He thinks he wanted a whole return for himself. What he needed was to understand, before he died, that a vessel can be repaired without first being emptied — the thing his son’s household’s foundling will one day prove in the face of every priest in Sarn. He did not live long enough to be wrong about it in an interesting way, which is why his absence still has work to do.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Sella Vail} — his wife. She decided he would not be collected; he did not ask her to decide. A promise unkept gathers Ash in Sarn as surely as a debt, and the ridge in their room is the shape of theirs. \emph{Ch 2:} she touches the widow’s mark before the kiln and at no other time; she will not let anyone fill his seat or take his coat down, and the house keeps a private roll in which he is entered and which it does not show to a man from Slate Row. \emph{Ch 3:} the mark she touches only before the kiln was confirmed by the factor at Greybank and entered at the Court as a kindness, and she did not answer Berr when he said it; she went on covering the boy’s ears against the river wind instead.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — his son, who has his hands and not his patience. Garl never told the boy he was proud of him. The boy has spent three years building a life around the absent sentence. \emph{Ch 2:} Jorra sits in the hollow seat he cannot fill and goes to the wheel when he is angry, which is the habit Garl had and never explained.

\textbf{Corin} — he never met the boy, who now sleeps under his roof and uses a warm stone that was warmed at Garl’s fire. There is a late recognition in this: the dead man’s hearth warmed the child he would take in, and a promise that cannot be settled because it was never made. \emph{Ch 2:} the boy read him as a man-shaped gap --- four, four and a space where the fifth thing should be --- and saw the charred edge of a slate in the kiln ash without knowing whose hand had written on it.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — owed Garl for a consignment of tiles and never entered it. Garl never called it in. The Vail house has been short of coin ever since, and the debt has gathered interest in the Court’s books even though nobody in Low Kiln can read them. \emph{Ch 3:} at the Cross the debt was spoken around and never named, a space in Berr’s sentence with the shape of the hollow seat; his care not to call it was another kindness entered nowhere, for a debt uncalled can never be paid, settled or done with.

\textbf{Enna} — his daughter, who died at nine months and whose second chill he could not stop. He has been uncollected for a year; she has been returned thin or not returned at all, which nobody in the house will say aloud. Both are entered on the house’s private roll.

\textbf{Ch 4.} His coals held under ash all night and the kiln kept its four and four and a gap; the seat stayed hollow, the salt coat kept its shape, the ridge turned the corner unswept and the charred slate lay in the ash unexamined. Sella touched the mark for the kiln and not at the bank, said his sayings to the fire and the pot, and let the debt against the tiles lie uncalled while a measure was called in another house. Jorra came in with slate dust and did not say what the back room held.

\textbf{Ch 5.} The house keeps his shape without needing him in it. On Stone 13 the coals held under their ash and the kiln took its breath in fours and missed one; on Stone 15 the coat kept its salt-stiff shape on the peg, the seat its hollow on the left, and the ridge ran to the corner and turned it without a break, unswept. His proverbs went to the fire and the pot --- \emph{feed the fire before you feed yourself} and \emph{leave the crack showing} --- and never to a person. The debt against the tiles stayed uncalled while another measure was called elsewhere, and he was not asked to settle either.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
He is an absence with objects attached, and in Chapter 2 the objects are given a room. The kiln breathes in fours with a gap where the fifth breath should be; the wheel-seat is worn hollow on the left by twenty years of leaning; a salt-stiff coat keeps a broader man’s shape on its peg behind the door; the Ash ridge runs the length of the skirting board and turns the corner without a break, near two thumbs wide, and nobody sweeps it before the Last Day. His sayings have outlived him and improved in the keeping, and the house says them to the pot and the fire and never to a person. In the kiln ash lies the charred edge of a slate with the writing gone off the end, swept around for a year and never picked up. In Chapter 3 the absence leaves the house and goes into the market: the kiln’s breath missed one as the boy was carried out, the uncalled debt against the tiles hangs in the space between Berr and Sella, and a barrow of the same grey Ash goes out through the north gate while nobody looks twice. The reader knows him by what the household and the district do around the hole before anyone says how the hole was made.

On Days 5 and 6 the absence keeps the house. The coals under their ash hold all night and the kiln goes on taking its breath in fours and a gap; the seat stays hollow on the left, the salt coat keeps its broad shape on its peg, and the Ash ridge along the skirting board is not swept. In the kiln ash the charred edge of a slate lies black on one side and eaten into the shape of a strip of bark, with the writing gone off the end, and nobody picks it up. His proverbs are said to the pot and the fire in his improved voice --- \emph{feed the fire before you feed yourself}, \emph{leave the crack showing} --- and never to a person. The debt against the tiles lies uncalled beside a measure that was called and entered, and the boy files the two facts together without joining them.

On Days 7, 8 and 9 the absence keeps the house as exactly as it did on Days 5 and 6: banked coals holding under ash, a breath missed in the fours, the coat, the seat and the unswept ridge, and the sayings improved by twenty years of use said to the clay and the fire. The boy now files the death-house bench and the interval of a note that did not come beside the older objects, and the uncalled debt beside the warmth entered nowhere, joining none of them. Garl stays dead; the house’s debt and the Ash ridge are the shape of the promise he never made and Sella never released.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Does not appear; his name, the stone warmed at his hearth and the house’s silence around him are held for Chapter 2.

\item Ch 2: Present throughout Days 2 and 3 as the shape of the house. The kiln’s breath he kept alive was counted in fours and a gap; his seat stayed hollow, his coat stayed on its peg, his Ash ridge grew, and his proverb \emph{leave the crack showing} was said in the house’s voice to the pot and the fire. The house’s private roll was spoken around: he was entered on it, and the foundling’s name would be entered there in a careful hand. The charred edge of a slate lay in the kiln ash and was not examined. No collector was called.

\item Ch 3: Day 4, Account-day. Present as what is left where it lay. The kiln took one breath behind the boy as he was carried out and missed the next; the lead on Berr’s breath and the red cord folded square came with the confirmation of Sella’s widow’s mark, and the debt Garl left against the tiles went spoken around, neither called nor settled. In the afternoon a barrow of grey Ash went out through the north gate toward the Bone Orchard, the same work that went out every market-day; the boy put it with the ridge under the Vail skirting board and the charred slate in the kiln ash, and nobody explained any of the three.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Days 5 and 6. Present as the shape of the house and its rule. The banked coals held under their ash through the night and the kiln breathed four, four and a gap; the hollow seat stayed unfilled, the salt-stiff coat stayed on its peg, and the Ash ridge ran to the corner and turned it without a break, unswept. The charred edge of the slate lay in the kiln ash and was not examined. His sayings went to the fire and the pot --- \emph{feed the fire before you feed yourself} and \emph{leave the crack showing} --- and nobody said them to the boy. The debt against the tiles lay uncalled while the year’s first measure was called and entered in another house, and the boy kept the two facts side by side.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Days 7--9. Present as the house’s shape and its rule. The coals held under ash through the night and the kiln took its breath in fours and a gap; the salt-stiff coat kept its shape on the peg behind the door, the wheel-seat its hollow on the left, and the Ash ridge ran to the corner and turned it without a break, unswept and not to be swept before the Last Day. His sayings went to the fire and the pot --- \emph{feed the fire before you feed yourself} and \emph{leave the crack showing; people trust a thing they can see has been handled} --- and never to a person. The debt against the tiles stayed uncalled while a number of days to Kindling went around the house as a closed fist.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Jorra Vail}{the wheel-keeper who has never said which side he is on}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Jorra, Jorra of the Vail, the wheel-keeper}\CRfield{Role}{family}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 796, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; House Vail}\CRfield{Allegiance}{House Vail kiln-house; Kiln-Masters of Sill; quietly courted by the Broken Slates}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 2}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Sixteen and built like a gate hinge: tall for a clay boy, narrow, with slip dried to the colour of old bone up both forearms and a permanent bend in his left little finger from a splinter off the wheel when he was eleven. He cuts his hair short so Ash cannot nest in it, which is a thing the district notices and he never explains. His eyes do not finish sentences; they go to the door, to the fire, to the wheel, and come back without reporting. He smells of damp clay, tallow and the sweetish edge of lead glaze carried up from High Kiln on a bad wind. The thing people notice first is his hands, which are the hands of a thrower and have been working since he was nine. The thing they notice last is that he never looks at Sella when she speaks to him, though he obeys her instantly.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Short, flat, declarative. He asks almost no questions, and when he does it is the tool rather than the clay: \emph{Is it dry? Did you fire it? Who wrote it?} He speaks to the wheel and to the pots as other men speak to animals, and only then to people. When he is angry he gets quieter, the way Sella does, and the household has learned to read the lowering of his voice as a weather signal. He never says \emph{with} or \emph{against} when \emph{I haven’t said} will do.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The wheel doesn’t care whose hands. It cares if they’re steady.”

\item “You want me to say it. I’m not saying it.”

\item “Sella. Don’t feed him from your own dish. He’s not Vail.”

\item “A slate burns in four minutes. Four. I’ve timed it.”

\item “I didn’t say I was with them. I said I wasn’t against them. That’s different. In this district it isn’t, but it’s what I said.”

\item “He’s not Vail.” --- said in the kiln-house at first light, refused an answer, and sealed by the boy’s arrival.

\item “You’ll do.” --- to the pot on the wheel, in a voice with no welcome in it and no unkindness either.

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He is the son of Garl and Sella Vail, and he took the wheel at thirteen when Garl died in the Long Cold and did not come back and nobody came to draw him. The wheel had been Garl’s for twenty years and the seat was worn hollow; Jorra has spent three years trying to fill that hollow with his own weight. He has thrown pots since he was nine, fired them since eleven, and knows the full note of a Vail pot before it leaves his hands, though he will not say so in front of the kiln-masters, who would call it early and make something of it. His house has remembered the Ash Rising since Y.H. 668, when children were taken and the rising was put down by a man with a list; the memory is kept not in stories but in the way certain doors are never closed and certain names are never entered. Since he was fourteen men from the Broken Slates have come into the back room of the kiln-house and spoken in low voices. He has listened to all of them and told none of them yes.

He watched Enna, his sister, die of the second chill at four, and has never forgiven the fire for being warm while she cooled. He watched his father die and was sent to find a collector and could not cross the ford, and has never asked whether he was sent too late or whether he was sent at all. When Corin arrived he said one sentence — \emph{He’s not Vail} — and has said nothing about it since, which in that house is a form of argument.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
A father who went uncollected, a sister who went cold in a warm room, and a wheel he was given before he was ready. He fears Sella will die with something unsaid and leave him holding the house and the lie together. He fears the Broken Slates will decide for him what he is, because he has not decided and the longer he waits the more their decision looks like his own. He fears the boy most of all, because the boy hears things. His contradiction: he is the most careful keeper of the Vail fire in four generations and he is quietly helping the people who want to burn every slate in Sill. He has never once thought these two facts cannot live in the same man.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the wheel to stay in the house and the fire to hold and nobody to write the boy down as Vail without asking him first. He thinks he wants Sella to say that he has done well with the wheel. What he needs is to learn that burning a slate does not return a person to you, and that the anger he has been nursing for three years is not his alone — it has been lent to him by men who intend to collect on it.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Sella Vail} — his mother. He believes she took Corin in to replace Enna and to keep the house’s standing after Garl died. She has never contradicted him. Every obedience he gives her is therefore double: it is love and it is a correction neither of them will make. \emph{Ch 2:} she announced the taking and he answered \emph{he’s not Vail}; she got quieter and gave him no answer at all. He went on working, did not look at her, and shifted the damper before she had finished telling him to.

\textbf{Corin} — the foundling. Jorra was the first in the house to notice that the boy turns his head toward a pot before anyone taps it, and that he does it to hear the note, not the noise. He has told nobody. He resents the boy for being given a bed in the house while the clay-bound hand sleeps in the loft, and he cannot stop watching him. \emph{Ch 2:} the foundling arrived under the roof and nobody wrote him as Vail, which is what Jorra wanted and what he cannot bear. He spoke to the wheel, to the pots and once to Tarn about a rasp out of its place, and never addressed the boy.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — attended Enna’s death and Corin’s birth, and has never met Jorra’s eye for longer than a moment. He knows she wrote something down the night Enna died and has never asked to see it, because asking would make it a thing between them.

\textbf{Tarn} — the Vail’s clay-bound hand. Jorra treats him as a brother when the wheel needs him and as a servant when it does not. Tarn has decided to hate him for it and keeps failing, because Jorra once sat up all night with him through a fever and has never mentioned it. \emph{Ch 2:} Tarn was given the loft while the foundling was given the bed, and said so in one sentence to Sella. Jorra said nothing; he has never defended the household’s arithmetic and never contests it.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — Jorra threw a stone at his door in Y.H. 810 and denied it. The Thane suspects the boy is with the Broken Slates and lets him be, which Jorra finds more frightening than being watched.

\textbf{Ch 4.} Sella’s hand went to the mark before the kiln and not at the bank; he shifted the damper before she had finished and again at dusk, and did not take up her silence about the back room. The boy was at the edge of his notice as a thing that hears: he spoke to the pots and not to him, and came in from the back room with slate dust worked into his forearms --- grey and fine, not slip --- and said \emph{the back room’s cold} to nobody. He wanted the boy outside the name Vail and the hollow seat his, and did not say either.

\textbf{Ch 5.} The wheel took the house into its rhythm again with slip dried to old bone up both forearms and no slate dust on Stone 13 or Stone 15; the boy noticed the absence and could not ask which it meant. He told a pot \emph{you’ll do}, told the wheel it did not care whose hands were on it, and shifted the damper before Sella had finished telling him the fire was low. He did not say the back room was cold. \emph{He’s not Vail} stayed sealed as it had been on the hour it was spoken, and the foundling went on being carried under the roof without a Vail name, which is what Jorra wanted and what he could not bear.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
He enters at the wheel with his weight in the hollow his father wore and has spent three years failing to fill --- slip dried to the colour of old bone up both forearms, the left little finger bent from a splinter at eleven, a story about a fight he tells because a fight sounds more like him. In Chapter 2 he says one flat sentence about the foundling and is refused an answer, and the sentence is sealed by the boy’s arrival: a child lies under the roof and nobody has written him as Vail. He does not look at his mother while obeying her instantly, speaks to the pots before people, goes quiet when the levy is spoken of, and has given none of the men who come into the back room a word they can carry away. He is the most careful keeper of the Vail fire in four generations and is quietly helping the people who want to burn every slate in Sill, and has never once thought the two facts cannot live in the same man.

On Days 5 and 6 the back room comes into the house on his forearms as slate dust, grey and fine, worked into the creases past the bend of the little finger, and not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel. He tells the pots \emph{you’ll do}, shifts the damper before Sella has finished telling him and shifts it again at dusk, and says \emph{the back room’s cold} to nobody in particular --- not an answer and not a question, shaped so nobody can take it further. Sella does not take it further. The boy watches the dust and wants to know which slate was broken, and whether it was a birth-slate, and keeps the questions where questions can last for years.

On Days 7, 8 and 9 the wheel keeps the house’s time and the absence of dust is as much a fact as its presence was on Stone 12. He works in bone-coloured slip, speaks to the pots and the wheel and never to the basket, and moves the damper before Sella has finished: an obedience that is also a correction neither will name. He goes quieter when the levy is spoken around and quieter still at \emph{the house’s share}, and the yard straightens around him. The broken birth-slates under the drying rack and the men whose names are not spoken stay unshown; the back room is not entered in any roll the house shows, and its cold is not mentioned. He still wants the foundling outside the name and the hollow seat for himself, and has said neither.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Does not appear; the wheel, the hollow seat and his objecting are held for Chapter 2.

\item Ch 2: At first light stood at the wheel with slip-coloured forearms and the bent finger, told the pot the slip was too thick and it would crack at the shoulder, and went quiet when the levy was spoken of. When Sella said the boy would come under the roof he said \emph{he’s not Vail}, did not look at her, and was not answered. On the third day sat in the hollow seat as the boy was carried in, obeyed Sella’s instruction about the damper before she had finished, told a pot \emph{you’ll do}, spoke once to Tarn about a rasp, and never addressed the child. Nobody wrote the foundling as Vail.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Days 5 and 6. At the wheel with slip dried to old bone on both forearms and the bent little finger, speaking to the pots and not to the boy, obeying Sella before she had finished and shifting the damper before she had finished again. At dusk came in from the back room with slate dust worked into the forearms --- grey and fine, not slip --- and said \emph{the back room’s cold} to nobody, answering no question about where he had been and giving Sella nothing to take further. Garl’s coat kept its salt shape on its peg and his hollow seat stayed unfilled; he did not look at the basket.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Days 7--9. At the wheel with slip dried to old bone up both forearms, the bent little finger and no slate dust on Stone 13 or Stone 15; told a pot \emph{you’ll do} and the wheel \emph{it doesn’t care whose hands}, and did not look toward the basket. Shifted the damper before Sella had finished telling him the fire was low and went quieter when the levy was spoken around. Did not say the back room was cold, did not take up Sella’s silence, and kept \emph{he’s not Vail} sealed while the foundling went on being carried under the roof unwritten.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Mira Silt}{the aunt who knows and will not say}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Mira, Mira of the Fishstairs, Dessa’s sister}\CRfield{Role}{family}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 785, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; returned thin)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; House Silt}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Fishstairs, Sill; eel trade; House Silt}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 1}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Twenty-seven, broad-hipped, with eel slime dried on her sleeves and a small smile she keeps on her face the way another woman keeps a door on the latch: it can be shut without anybody noticing the movement. The top half of her left ear is missing, taken by a knife in a Fishstairs quarrel when she was nineteen; she says a hook did it, and the scar says otherwise, and nobody has ever made anything of it. She smells of river silt, salted perch, tallow smoke and the cold off the water. She carries a string of eel-bones in her pocket and flicks them against her thumb when she is deciding whether to lie, which is often. The thing people notice first is the smile; the thing they notice last is that she never sits down at a table where a slate is open.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Fast, low, with the proverb turned sideways. She answers a question with a question and then answers it herself, which gives her the room to choose which answer the listener gets. She never tells a story with a beginning; she starts at the part where somebody is already in trouble. She has learned from Fen traders to mark accounts in knots rather than on slate, and will not write a name down if a knot will hold it. When she is being kind she becomes brisk, because kindness in her house is a thing done quickly before anyone can make a speech about it.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “You want to know. You don’t want to know. I can see which one’s winning.”

\item “The secret’s in what happens to it after. I didn’t tell anybody anything. There’s nothing to tell. That’s the whole of it.”

\item “It came from a warm house, that’s all. There’s barley in it and a little smoke and it’s a crust, not a sermon.”

\item “Nineteen steps. Your sister counts them. I count the ones she doesn’t take.”

\item “Don’t ask a Fishstairs woman what she saw. She’ll sell it to you and then it’s yours and you’ll have to carry it.”

\item “He’s warm. That’s all I’ll say on a slate, and I’m saying it off the slate.”

\item “I kept your secret for three years, Dessa. Don’t make it a fourth.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
Born in Low Kiln to the same thin-returned mother as Dessa, Mira learned the eel trade at the Fishstairs at twelve and has worked the dawn market there ever since. She knows which households pay and which owe, which collectors take their ale before a collection and which after, and which boats on the Ilm are carrying something other than fish. She has been the district’s informal ledger of who is warm and who is cooling for fifteen years, and has never once put a name on paper.

She was in the room on Stone 7, Y.H. 812, when her sister bore the child with the branching red flower. She saw Sella Vail carry a covered bundle up the Clay Yard three days later, and she understood what had been done without being told. Alda Reed told Dessa the boy was among the Forty; Mira let her believe it. She has let her believe it for three years, and has taken a share of the barley Sella pays every winter, which she spends on Dessa, who does not know where the barley comes from. She has a child of her own, returned thin in Y.H. 802 to a household in Nine Pots, who does not recognize her; she has walked to Nine Pots twice and never knocked.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Her sister’s lie is hers too, and she has begun to feel the weight of it in the hand that holds the eel knife. She fears that telling Dessa the truth will put the boy on a slate that someone can use against him; she fears more that if she waits much longer the truth will come out in a quarrel, which is the worst way for a thing like that to be said. She fears she has become a woman who trades in other people’s warmth and calls it looking after them. Her contradiction: she gossips all day at the Fishstairs and has kept the one secret that matters for three years without a single slip.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants Dessa to stop reaching for a child she believes is dead, and she wants to be believed once about something that is not a rumour. She thinks she wants the truth to come out, and has arranged her life so that it never has to. What she needs is to understand that a secret kept out of love is still a debt, and that it gathers Ash like any other debt, and that the only way to stop paying interest on it is to say the thing.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Dessa Silt} — her sister, her room-mate, the woman whose child she has hidden for three years. Dessa suspects; Mira has begun to be afraid of the moment Dessa stops suspecting and knows. Everything between them is said in the language of barley, coughs and the nineteen steps. \emph{Ch 1:} she crossed the room and pressed a crust of Vail-smoked barley into Dessa’s hand in two brisk movements, stopping short of the slate to do it.

\textbf{Corin} — the nephew she watches from a distance at the market and once followed as far as the Clay Yard. She has never called him anything, not even in her head, because a name is a thing that can be overheard. \emph{Ch 1:} she would not hold him and would not sit where the slate lay open, but pushed the wool back under him briskly with two movements before anyone could make a speech about it. \emph{Ch 3:} a background figure at the eel stall in the afternoon, eel slime dried pale on her sleeves and the top of her left ear gone; she flicked an eel-bone against her thumb and never once looked at the basket. When she turned to go she shoved a loose board clear of the barrow men’s path with the side of her hand, brisk, her face still toward the eels. She had not looked at him once all day. He put her not-looking beside the man on the block’s: one priced him, the other kept him, and he had no word in Grathic for either.

\textbf{Sella Vail} — they have an arrangement: barley and silence. Mira has begun to resent the barley and has never once refused it, which is the precise shape of her complicity. \emph{Ch 1:} she watched the hand in the door without moving, her thumb quiet in her pocket and the eel-bone put away.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — they trade small facts and never the large one. Alda knows more about the birth; Mira knows more about what happened after it. Neither will sit at a table with a slate on it, and they have built a friendship entirely out of things said standing up. \emph{Ch 1:} they worked the same room standing, each with her back to the writing.

\textbf{Orra Weir} — her supplier at the Weir and her oldest friend, who wants the secret out and has said so twice. Mira has answered both times with the weather.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — buys her eels for the levy feast and pays in marks that are short by two. She short-weights him by two eels. This small war has been going on for six years and neither has acknowledged it. \emph{Ch 3:} at the eel stall she asked him the price and answered it herself --- \emph{two marks the pair or two marks the pair and two short? Don’t trouble yourself. It’s two marks the pair and two short, and I’ve counted them twice, and I’ll count them again after I’ve eaten.} He did not answer, because she had left him nothing in the sentence that wanted an answer. She moved an eel-bone from hand to hand and said a figure under her breath. The knot-string is still not shown; only the counting is.

\textbf{Tarn} — the Vail’s clay-bound hand. She has suspected for a year that he is more than the debt he says he is, and has not asked, because she recognizes a person who keeps a secret and knows better than to open him.

\textbf{Ch 5.} At the Fishstairs on Stone 14 and the market’s edge on Stone 15 she worked with pale slime on her sleeves and silt in her neck, head held a little to the right against the cold where the ear had been. She flicked three eel-bones against her thumb while deciding whether to lie and put one away when the deciding was done; she gave Berr four eels where six should go against his two marks short of six years, and neither acknowledged the war. Asked whose the basket was, she answered with a question and then her own: \emph{he’s warm} --- all she would say on a slate, and she was saying it off the slate. Her grey wool cord at the waist was touched three times in two mornings when a number was spoken, and the second cord twisted twice beneath it has never gone up the river to Nine Pots and has never been lifted at a door; the thumb goes always to the first. She put a brown-edged crust into the fold briskly, called it a crust and not a sermon, and went back to the eels without looking in. She has not said she knows the child is Dessa’s.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
She is in the birth room from the first hours of Day 1 and is already arranged: the smile kept on the face like a door on the latch, the back turned to the slate, the eel-bones flicked and put away. She says the one true thing in the room --- that the secret is in what happens to a child after it is born --- in a form that can be taken as nothing, and follows it with \emph{there’s nothing to tell}. In Chapter 3 she is seen for the first time in her own element, the eel stall at the Cross, and the arrangement holds: dried slime, the missing top of her left ear, a question asked and answered by herself, a board shoved clear for the barrow men, and not one look into the basket all afternoon. The knot-string and the barley account are still offstage; the woman who knows and will not say is not. Her marker is reconciled to Chapter 1: she is on stage here, not first seen at the Fishstairs in Chapter 5.

On Days 8 and 9 the woman who knows is seen in her own element for two mornings, and the keeping becomes a craft with tools: eel-bones moved while an answer is chosen, a cord touched when a figure is spoken, a second cord kept apart and never taken to the door it belongs to. The little war with Berr is given its exact arithmetic --- four eels for six, two marks short for six years --- and neither will name it. She answers the question about the basket without letting the sentence want a reply, and gives the warmth briskly before anyone can make a speech about it, which is how kindness is done in her house. The knot-string itself is still not shown plainly and the knots are not counted; what the boy sees is the hand, the movement and the difference between two cords, and he has no word for any of it.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Came in from the yard with eel slime dried on her sleeves and the cold on them; stood in the one patch of floor the slate did not reach, back to the writing and face to the door. Answered a question with a question and then her own answer: the secret is in what happens to a child after. Said she had told nobody and there was nothing to tell. Flicked an eel-bone against her thumb and put it away when the hand came; kept the small smile on the latch all night. Crossed the room to press a crust of brown-edged Vail-smoked barley bread into Dessa’s hand, briskly, stopping short of the slate. The top of her left ear is missing --- a hook, she says; the scar says a knife.

\item Ch 3: Day 4, Account-day. Background figure at the eel stall at the edge of the Cross in the afternoon, with the top of her left ear gone and eel slime dried pale on her sleeves; kept her head a little to the right against the cold in the place where the ear had been. Flicked eel-bones against her thumb, asked Berr the eel price and answered it herself with \emph{two marks the pair and two short}, said a figure under her breath and moved another bone. Shoved a loose board clear of the barrow men’s path with the side of her hand and went on talking as if she had moved nothing. Never looked at the basket once all day.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Days 8 and 9. At the eel board below the north bridge with slime dried pale on her sleeves, silt in her neck and the top of her left ear gone; head held to the right against the cold. Flicked three eel-bones against her thumb while deciding whether to lie and put one away when the deciding was done; gave Berr four eels where six should go against his two marks short for six years, and did not acknowledge it. Answered a question about the basket with a question and her own answer --- \emph{he’s warm}, off the slate --- and shifted her back to the corner of his open slate rather than look at it. Her hand went to the grey cord at her waist three times in two mornings when a figure was spoken, never to the second cord twisted twice beneath it, which has never been carried up the river to Nine Pots. On Stone 15 put a brown-edged barley crust into the fold at the boy’s side in two brisk movements, called it a crust and not a sermon, and went back to the eels. Never said she knew him.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Sella Vail}{the woman who keeps the fire and asks no questions}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Sella, the Vail widow, Widow Vail, the keeper of the fire}\CRfield{Role}{family}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 779, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; born to a family of slip-mixers}\CRfield{Allegiance}{House Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln; Kiln-Masters of Sill by widow’s mark}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 1}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
A broad woman of thirty-three with forearms the colour of dried slip and a burn across the back of her left hand shaped like a little door --- two straight sides, a lintel and a threshold, the skin inside it puckered and pale --- which she keeps half-closed when she carries a cup, and which closes on nothing when her hand has nothing to hold. She is not tall and she does not make herself tall; she takes up room by being impossible to move. Her hair is coal-black with one grey streak at the temple that she says came from the winter Garl died and will not discuss further. She smells of woodsmoke, wet wool, and under it the faint sour mineral tang of the Ash ridge along the skirting board, which she has not swept in two years and does not intend to sweep. Her hands are never still. When they have nothing to do they count: spoons, nails, the breaths of a sleeping child. She wears her widow’s mark on a cord at her throat, a thumb-length of lead-glazed clay with the Vail kiln scratched into it, and she touches it before she opens the kiln and never at any other time. People notice the burn first and the mark last, which is the order she prefers.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Grathic proverbs and half-sentences, practical instruction addressed to the pot or the fire before it is addressed to a person. She says \emph{the house} where another woman would say \emph{I}, and \emph{the boy} where another would say \emph{my son}. She never finishes a sentence that would require her to say what she feels, and she is not unaware of this; she has simply decided that a thing said aloud becomes a thing the Court can enter. Her humour is dry and costs her something. When she is angry she gets quieter, and the quieter she gets the more the household straightens up.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The fire doesn’t know you’re sorry. Feed it.”

\item “Don’t set that near the door. You’ll pay for it in the thaw.”

\item “He’s a boy. He’s warm. That’s the whole of what I know.”

\item “You can ask me what you like. I won’t answer it. Ask the pot instead; it’s more use.”

\item “A slate-name with no house is a coat anybody can wear. I’ve seen it before.”

\item “The boy comes under the roof on the morrow. The house will keep him.” --- the taking announced without an \emph{I} in it, and never explained.

\item “Don’t set that near the door. You’ll pay for it in the thaw.” --- instruction to the pot, not to a person, given with the kiln already open.

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born in Low Kiln to slip-mixers, married Garl Vail at seventeen, and kept the Vail fire ever since in the way a widow keeps a wound clean: daily, without comment, with a hand that knows exactly how much pressure the thing will bear. She bore two children. Jorra, who lived, and Enna, who died at nine months of the second chill in Y.H. 804, after Alda Reed had warmed the stone and done everything the craft knew. Sella sat up with that child for three nights and learned what the midwives already knew: that a vessel can cool while everybody is being kind to it. In Y.H. 811, in the Long Cold, Garl died with the river iced and no collector able to cross, and his warmth went where unkept warmth goes. The Ash in the Vail corner is thicker than in any other house in the district. Sella has never swept it and has never said why.

On Stone 7, Y.H. 812, a woman in the district bore a child with a branching red flower across his ribs, and Sella took him in on the third day. She gave as her reason the one she has given ever since: a foundling with no house-name can be used by anyone, and a boy entered under a roof is a boy nobody can take for a levy without answering to that roof. This is true. It is not the whole of it. Alda Reed, who delivered him, crossed herself at the birth-flower and told nobody what she had seen, and Sella, who saw the same look on the midwife’s face, asked her nothing at all. She has kept the boy close for three years and has never once asked where his memory came from. She is waiting to see what he will become.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Enna’s second chill; Garl going uncollected; the Ash ridge that has grown an inch since. She fears another Long Cold, and fears more than that the day somebody from Slate Row notices that one child in her house listens to the fire before he looks at it. She fears Jorra, who has begun to disappear into the back rooms with men whose names are not spoken, and she fears that she has already lost the wheel to him while he still stands at it. Her contradiction is the whole of her: the woman who feeds every hungry child in the yard has never once said she loves one. She would sooner swallow the widow’s mark.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants the fire to hold, the wheel to stay in the house, and a Vail name written on the birth-slate of every child born under her roof. She thinks she wants a whole return for Garl, so that he might come back as himself and sit where he used to sit and say nothing in his own voice. What she needs is to admit that she did not take Corin in for the house at all. She took him because he was the only warm thing in a room full of people pretending not to be cold, and because she had already lost one child to the second chill and could not bear to lose another to an explanation.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Jorra Vail} — her son, the keeper of the wheel. He believes she took the foundling in to replace Enna and to keep the house’s standing while he did the work. She has never told him otherwise, which is how he has come to believe it. They speak about the kiln, the levy, the weather; they do not speak about Garl, or Enna, or the boy. \emph{Ch 2:} he said \emph{he’s not Vail} when she announced the taking, and she did not answer him; she grew quieter and the house straightened up around it. He went on turning the wheel without looking at her and obeyed her before she had finished. The sentence stands and the boy is under the roof and still unwritten.

\textbf{Corin} — the foundling she took in and has never questioned. She watches him with a hearth-maid’s attention and a widow’s refusal to name what she sees. Her silence is its own kind of Court: kindness that does not want an answer, and that therefore cannot be refused. \emph{Ch 1:} her hand came in ahead of her, felt for the heat at his belly, found none, opened and shut once over the covered bundle, and withdrew. She did not look at him, or at any of the women, and said nothing. \emph{Ch 2:} the hand that hovered now takes. She crossed to the bench first and put her hand near the slate as near a sleeping animal, then lifted him, the little-door burn closing first on nothing and then on his weight. She said \emph{the boy} and \emph{the house}, carried him up the yard without looking back, laid him on fresh straw, and gave instruction to the pot and the fire. She has not written him as Vail and has said nothing of payment or purpose. \emph{Ch 3:} she carried him out under the Vail roof for the first time, in a basket under a fold of wool, like a pot filled that must not be slopped. She kept a half-pace from every open slate, put the fold back without looking, answered a question about whose house with \emph{the house}, and answered a question about the mother with \emph{Account-day}. All morning she spoke to the wool and the wind: \emph{Keep it off his ears. It’s the river wind.} She did not look into the basket on the walk home and said nothing of what had been read over him.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — delivered Enna and Corin both, and crossed herself at the second birth. They have traded one silence for three years. Alda has the fuller notes; Sella has the fuller knowledge of what a silence costs when a household must eat. \emph{Ch 1:} Alda laid the slate flat on the bench as Sella’s hand went back out the door; the trade of silences begins in that room. \emph{Ch 2:} Sella put her hand near the writing on the bench as she took the child and did not touch it, and Alda did not look at her. The arrangement holds without a word passing between them.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — the woman who bore the boy. Sella knows it and has paid her in barley and slip-clay to stay away, and has never entered the payment in any account, not even the private one she keeps in her head. \emph{Ch 1:} Dessa’s pulse rose under her \emph{door shut} smile while the hand hung above her child; neither looked at the other. \emph{Ch 2:} Sella took the boy up the yard without once looking at the woman standing at the end of the nineteen steps, and Dessa did not follow.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — confirmed her widow’s mark and has never called in the debt Garl left against a shipment of tiles. The uncalled debt is worse than a called one, because she cannot pay it and cannot settle it and cannot stop owing it. \emph{Ch 3:} at the Slate Row end of the Cross he told her the mark was confirmed by the factor at Greybank, that it was a kindness, and that kindness is entered. She did not answer him about the mark; she pulled the wool up over the boy’s ear and said it was the river wind. He stood a moment with his mouth open on the next part, then left the debt where it lay as carefully as he had found it. She has never asked and has never thanked him, which he has decided means she knows.

\textbf{Garl Vail} — her husband, uncollected in the Long Cold. She decided he would not be collected; he did not ask her to decide. The Ash in the corner is the shape of that decision and she has lived beside it for a year. \emph{Ch 2:} she keeps his seat empty and his coat on its peg, will not let anyone sweep the ridge, and touches the widow’s mark only before she opens the kiln. The house keeps a roll of its own in which he is entered, and does not show it to a man from Slate Row.

\textbf{Ch 4.} The boy was carried to the Drawing and back like a pot filled that must not be slopped; she placed him where he could see and not join and stood a half-pace off, her burn shut on nothing, and would not be the one to tell him what the room was doing. She did not answer Jorra’s \emph{the back room’s cold}; she shifted the damper. With Alda, a half-pace and a turned slate stood in for the arrangement, and neither asked what the other had written. Dessa at her threshold got no look from her and no word, and the barley between the two houses went on being unentered. Garl’s mark was touched before the kiln and not at the bank, and his coat, seat, ridge and proverb kept the house in his shape while the debt stayed uncalled.

\textbf{Ch 5.} The rule of the mark held exactly: touched before the opening on Stone 13 and at no other time, not at the bank that night or on Stone 15. She fed him barley gruel from the house’s own dish, holding his head with two fingers behind the ear, and gave every instruction to the fire, the pot and the wool --- \emph{keep it off his ears; it’s the river wind} --- never to the basket. She carried him down to the Fishstairs twice and to the market’s edge once, kept her half-pace from every open slate, laid marks for two pair of eels and counted them with her eyes, and said nothing about the collector’s cuff, the cord or where they had been. At dusk on Stone 15 her hand did not go to the mark; she counted loaves and bits with her lips and came back to the same place twice, keeping the shape of the days to Kindling without giving the number. She neither saw nor refused the crust Mira put in the fold, and where she is concerned these are the same thing.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
In Chapter 1 she is a hand in a doorway and a shadow, not a speaking part. Woodsmoke and wet wool arrive ahead of her; then the burn shaped like a little door reaches for the heat at the child’s belly, finds none, hovers over the bundle and withdraws. In Chapter 2 she is inside the house for the first time on the page and is known by its objects before she is known by her words: the hollow seat, the salt coat, the Ash ridge a thumb and then two thumbs wide, the charred edge of a slate nobody picks up, the roll the house keeps and does not show. She announces the taking, is answered by her son, refuses the answer by getting quieter, and on the third day performs the taking with a hand that knows how much a thing will bear. She lays the boy on a bed and tells the fire what to do. In Chapter 3 she takes that keeping out of the house for the first time and into a market that explains him as a figure. She carries him in a basket, keeps her half-pace from every open slate, refuses to say whose house he is beyond \emph{the house}, refuses to answer Berr about the mark, and walks him home without a word about where they have been. The private roll and the Court’s slate are seen to be the same shape of account, both entering a child who has not agreed to either, and her little-door hand is at one end of it. She has not explained any of it. Her silence has stopped being a pause and become a wall, and the wall has acquired a second face in the district’s grammar of entered kindness.

On Days 5 and 6 the keeping goes on without an entry. She touches the widow’s mark before the kiln on Stone 11 and at no other time, banks the coals with the back of a broken pot at dusk on Stone 12 and does not touch the mark then; she feeds the boy from her own dish and puts the fold right without looking at his face. She carries him to the Drawing and places the basket where he can see the work and cannot be part of it, never where a slate lies open, and stands a half-pace off with her little-door burn shut on nothing, refusing by her stillness to tell him what is being done. She says nothing about where they went, what was taken or from whom; when Jorra says \emph{the back room’s cold} she shifts the damper instead of answering. The wall has acquired a door she will not open and a room she will not enter, and the fire inside the rule of the mark goes on holding.

On Days 7, 8 and 9 the keeping goes on without an entry and adds no explanation. She opens the kiln under a hand that touches the mark and lets it go, feeds the foundling from the house’s dish and says \emph{the house} where another woman would say \emph{I}, and puts the fold right without looking at his face. She carries him down twice through the cold off the Slip Brook and sets him in the bridge’s shade on a patch no slate has reached, the same care she used at the edge of the light at the Drawing; she stands a half-pace from every open slate and buys eels without counting them with her fingers. At the bank she does not touch the mark, and at the end of Stone 15 she goes around the days to Kindling in the arrangement of loaves and bits and comes back to the same place twice, so that the number need never be said. The wall has grown a second face: it keeps out the woman at the eels, the crust in the fold, the cuff on the bridge and the count her son will not speak, and it keeps the boy warm without ever entering the warmth.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Did not enter the birth room and said nothing. Woodsmoke and wet wool came ahead of her, then the cold off the yard, then the hand with the little-door burn across its back --- lintel, threshold, two straight sides. It felt for heat at the child’s belly, found none, opened and shut once above the covered bundle and withdrew. She stepped back out into the Clay Yard without looking at child or women. The crust and the warmed stone had already come from her hearth, neither entered against anybody; the kiln breathed through the wall afterward. She still intends to take him on the third day.

\item Ch 2: Kept the fire and the house through Days 2 and 3. At first light touched the widow’s mark before opening the kiln and at no other time, gave instruction to the pot and the fire, would not let anyone fill Garl’s seat or take his coat down, and said the house kept a roll of its own. Announced that the boy would come under the roof and was answered \emph{he’s not Vail}; got quieter and did not reply. On the third morning came for him, crossed to the bench first, lifted him with the little-door hand, said \emph{the boy} and \emph{the house}, carried him up the Clay Yard and laid him on fresh straw. Gave the bed to the foundling and the loft to Tarn without explaining either, told the pot it would pay for itself in the thaw, and left the charred slate in the kiln ash untouched.

\item Ch 3: Day 4, Account-day. Carried the boy out of the kiln-house in a basket under a fold of wool --- he left the house and its heat went out behind him --- and held him like a pot filled that must not be slopped. Did not touch the widow’s mark, since the coals were only being banked. Counted off the question of the mother with \emph{Account-day} and the question of the house with \emph{the house}; put the fold back over his face and ears without looking and spoke the instruction to the wool and the wind. Kept a half-pace from the slate on the block and did not set the basket where it lay open. At the Slate Row end refused to answer Berr about the confirmed widow’s mark, pulled the wool over the boy’s ear, and let the uncalled debt lie. Lifted the basket off the post in the late afternoon and turned back up the Clay Yard without a word about where they had been or what had been read over the top of him; the wool smelled of woodsmoke and the door did not quite shut.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Days 5 and 6. On Stone 11 touched the widow’s mark before opening the kiln and at no other time, fed the household from her own dish, put the fold right without looking and gave the instruction about the draught to the fire. On Stone 12 carried the boy two doors down to a neighbour’s house for the Drawing, turned the basket with her knee and set it at the edge of the light, not where the slate lay open, and said \emph{a pot filled is a pot carried} to the girl at the hearth. Stood a half-pace from bench and bed with her burn shut on nothing and did not look toward the basket when the man gave. At dusk banked the coals under ash with the back of a broken pot, said \emph{that’ll hold} to the fire, put the fold right without looking at his face, and said nothing about where they had been, what had been taken or from whom. Did not touch the mark at the bank. Did not answer Jorra’s \emph{the back room’s cold}; shifted the damper instead. Fed the boy gruel thinned with milk from the house’s own dish and entered neither the spoon nor the boy.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Days 7--9. Touched the widow’s mark before opening the kiln on Stone 13 and at no other time, held it a moment longer than the touch required, and gave the instruction to the fire. Fed the boy barley gruel thinned with milk from the house’s own dish, spoon wiped on her sleeve, and said \emph{the house}, never \emph{I}; put the fold right at his ear and under his chin without looking at his face and did not wipe the drool. At dusk banked the coals with the back of a broken pot and did not touch the mark. Carried him down to the Fishstairs on Stone 8 and to the market’s edge on Stone 9, kept the half-pace from open slates and the shade where no slate lay, laid marks for two pair of eels and counted them with her eyes, and said nothing about the cuff, the cord, the crust or where they had been. On Stone 15 counted her loaves and bits with her lips twice and returned to the same place, keeping the days to Kindling as a shape and not a number; neither thanked nor refused the crust and did not take it out.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Fenna Clay}{the girl in High Kiln who mixed the glaze and knew the note}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Fenna, Fenna Clay, Fenna of the Glaze Room, the green girl}\CRfield{Role}{love-interest}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 800, High Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{High Kiln, Sill, Grath; House Clay, glaze-mixers for four generations}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Glaze Room, High Kiln; the kiln-masters’ household; a widow’s mark held in trust}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 40}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Twelve when she first appears and in the years after a woman of moderate height with the pale-green staining of lead glaze at the corners of her mouth that the district calls \emph{the green} and that she has never tried to hide, because a glaze-mixer without the green is a glaze-mixer who does not taste her work. Her hands are stained blue-green to the second knuckle and the stain is in the creases permanently, so that when she holds a cup at the Cross people look at the cup and not at her. She has a single lock of hair that never takes the dye of the kiln smoke and stays the colour of dry straw, which the High Kiln children say means she will marry someone from the lowlands, which she has spent years trying to disprove. She smells of lead glaze, crushed galena, vinegar and the sweetish heat of the Glaze Room in Ember, which is a beautiful smell and a poisonous one, and she is the only person in Sill who will tell you so in the same breath. The thing people notice first is the green at her mouth; the thing they notice last is that she taps everything — cups, tables, the wheel-shed frame when she visits — with a knuckle, and listens, and has never been able to stop.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Practical, dry, with the glaze-mixer’s habit of naming a thing by what it is made of rather than what it is: not \emph{a cup} but \emph{a lead cup}, not \emph{a lie} but \emph{a glaze over a crack}. She has a way of saying the dangerous thing in the vocabulary of the workshop, so that it can be taken as instruction if the listener prefers, which is the same defence Dessa Silt uses with different materials. She does not giggle and does not lower her eyes, which in High Kiln marks her as odd and in Low Kiln marks her as strange in a way that draws the boy from the yard across the river. When she is frightened she becomes technical, giving proportions and temperatures, because a recipe is a thing that holds still. She will not say \emph{please}, because her mother taught her that a glaze-mixer who begs gets the lead order wrong.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “It’s lead. The pale line at your gum is lead. I can tell you when it started, and you won’t thank me for it.”

\item “You tapped that before I did. Why did you tap it before I did? Nobody taps before the glaze-mixer.”

\item “A good glaze hides the crack. That’s its whole purpose and its whole defect. Ask a potter which he wants and he’ll lie to you.”

\item “I’m from High Kiln. You’re from the yard. If anybody writes it down it becomes a thing with a number on it, and I don’t want a number.”

\item “Don’t set it near the fire. I know you know. I want to hear you say it in our house’s words, not theirs.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born in High Kiln to a family of glaze-mixers who have supplied the Vail kiln-house and half the tables of Sill for four generations, which means her household is comfortable and quietly hated. She learned the trade at nine by tasting the mix the way her mother did and by tapping a fired pot with a knuckle to hear whether the glaze had filled the flaw or only covered it. She is good at it — better than her mother, which nobody in High Kiln will say aloud — and has a habit the masters call \emph{the ear}, which she has inherited from a grandmother nobody remembers and which she considers a recipe like any other: a thing that can be learned by doing it wrongly often enough.

She met Corin at the market when she was twelve and he was nine, when he turned his head toward a cup on her family’s table before she tapped it, and said, in the flat way of the yard, that it would crack at the handle in a season. She tapped it. It answered flat. She has never forgotten the exact sound, or the fact that he did not wait to be asked. They have spoken at the Cross, at the Fishstairs, at the edge of the Clay Yard which she is not supposed to cross, for years, in the intervals when a child from High Kiln and a foundling from Low Kiln can be seen together without anybody making a number of it. She has never called him Corin in front of anyone; she calls him \emph{the one who taps early}, and he has never corrected her.

When they are both older she will be the person who can tell him what a glaze does to a crack and why the Court’s fine ware is beautiful, which is the physical answer to the question of why the rich are beautiful and repeated. She will also be the person who refuses to be his if it means being entered on a slate, which is the first time he will meet a love that will not be booked. She is, in her own account, a woman who has spent her life covering flaws and has never once mistaken the covering for the repair.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
The green at her mouth is the beginning of sweet-sickness, which she knows, and she has told no one in her house, because a glaze-mixer who admits the sickness loses the wheel and her mother’s table. She fears that the boy from the yard will one day be entered in a Court roll and become a person with a number, and that she will be unable to look at him in the same way. She fears her own talent: she is better at hiding cracks than anyone in Sill, which is a useful trade and a way of life built on a lie that kills slowly. Her contradiction: the girl who insists that a glaze hides nothing has spent every day of her life hiding the pale line at her own gum, and calls it \emph{the green}, which is what everyone calls it, which is how Sarn makes a sickness into a trade-mark.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants to mix a glaze that does not poison the mixer, which is an impossibility in Sill and the reason she will eventually leave for Vhalen, where the glass men use different fluxes and do not taste their work. She thinks she wants the boy from the yard to say the thing he never says. What she needs is to understand that she cannot be kept whole by someone who is himself cracked, and that loving a mender is not the same as being mended, and that the crack that is visible is the only honest part of either of them. Their romance can only begin in adulthood, when both have survived their first firings and the green at her mouth has become a thing they can speak of plainly.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the boy who tapped early. The bond is a shared ear and a river between two districts; she has the note by inheritance, he has it by a life he cannot explain, and neither has ever asked the other where it came from, which is how they have kept it safe. The tension is class and the slate: she has a house-name and he does not, and a marriage between them would be an entry in a roll, which is the last thing either of them can afford.

\textbf{Sella Vail} — buys her family’s glaze and has never once paid the full price, which Fenna has decided is a kindness from a woman who cannot afford to be kind, which makes it worse. Sella has also never once asked why the girl comes to the edge of the yard, which Fenna has decided is the same kindness from the opposite direction.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — whom she has known all her life and who would sooner burn a slate than let a High Kiln girl into the wheel-shed. Jorra has never said why, and Fenna has decided it is because he is afraid she will hear the wheel too, which is true and not the whole of it.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — the Fishstairs woman who sells her eels and knows exactly what a High Kiln girl is doing at the edge of the Clay Yard. Mira has never said a word to her about it, which Fenna finds more frightening than if she had.

\textbf{Her mother} — who taught her to taste the mix and never once asked whether she had begun to taste it too often. The mother’s silence is the trade passed down: a woman who stops asking questions is a woman whose daughter will keep the wheel.

\textbf{Veno Brine} — the Greybank factor who buys High Kiln ware and once offered her a place in Vhal, which she refused without knowing why, and has since understood had something to do with the lead and a man who wanted her ear for a different trade.

\textbf{Ch 5.} She is on the page before she is named: a silhouette across the market’s edge on Stone 15 with the pale green of lead glaze at the corner of her mouth, standing among a row of pots and not looking into the basket in the bridge’s shade. No dialogue and no name on the page. The boy registers the green and files it among the objects he cannot enter, without knowing that it is a sickness called a mark of skill or that the girl who wears it will one day be the third ear.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
She is on the page in Chapter 5 before she is named: a green-mouthed silhouette across the market where the boy is set in the bridge’s shade, looking at pots and saying nothing, filed among the things he cannot enter. She enters in her own right at the Cross in Chapter 40, tapping a cup with a knuckle and being answered by a foundling who did not wait to be asked, which is the first time in the book that anybody hears the same note as he does --- the first ear he meets in a person, years after the green was planted in silence.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: A silhouette only across the market’s edge on Stone 15 --- green of lead glaze at the corner of her mouth, standing among the pots and not looking into the basket. No dialogue and no name on the page; the boy filed the green without knowing what it was. Her speaking debut stays planned for Ch 40 (the Cross, the cup that cracks at the handle).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Pia of the Quay}{the salt girl who never called anything a gift}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Pia, Pia of the Quay, Pia Quay, the girl with the tide-name}\CRfield{Role}{love-interest}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 798, Vhal, the Sallow coast}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Vhaleni)}\CRfield{Origin}{Vhal, the Sallow coast; a boat-owning house on the Quay}\CRfield{Allegiance}{her mother’s boat-share; the salt pans; the Council’s counting house in bad weather}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 90}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Fourteen when the Salt Road first brings her to Sill and later a woman of strong, compact build with salt-bleached hair and the sibilant Vhaleni way of holding a sentence: she lets the ends of words fall away, so that a listener in Grath hears half a thought and supplies the rest, which she has learned to use. Her hands are roughened by salt-paper and rope, and she has a small white scar across one knuckle from a fish-hook that went in at an angle, which she says was a gull and was not. She dresses in Vhaleni blue when the weather allows and in whatever is warm when it does not, and unlike the Grathic girls she does not cover her hair in a mill, which makes people at the Cross look twice and then look away. She smells of salt, wet rope, tar and the cold off the Sallow, which in Sill is either exotic or suspicious depending on who is doing the smelling. The thing people notice first is that she never takes off her shoes indoors, which in Vhal is ordinary and in Grath is an insult; the thing they notice last is that she counts coins by touch without looking and is almost always right, which is the same trick the eel women use with knots and which nobody in Sill has ever seen a visitor do.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Sibilant, practical, with tide-words where a Grathic person would use weather or God. She says \emph{the ebb}, \emph{the draw}, \emph{the turn}, and has no word for \emph{please} because Vhaleni has no word for a guest and therefore no word for asking. She makes contracts of everything, including kindness: a meal is entered, a night’s shelter is entered, and if she ever does something for nothing she says so at once so that the other person will know it was not a favour, because a favour in Vhalen is a debt with no date on it. She never explains a joke and never repeats herself. When she wants to say something tender she names a tide that has not happened yet, which is the closest Vhaleni gets to a promise.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Salt. You pay in salt or you pay in marks. Which is it? Don’t say \emph{kindly}. Kindly is the coin we don’t take.”

\item “The ebb’s at the fourth hour. Anything said before the ebb can be said again after it. I’ll let you.”

\item “A contract that rots in the rain is no contract. That’s the virtue of it. A thing that lasts too long stops earning.”

\item “You’re not a guest. I don’t have a word for guest. You’re a man whose account is current. That’s all I’ve got, and it’s better than it sounds.”

\item “I’ll name you a tide I haven’t seen. That’s what we do when we mean something we can’t write down.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born in Vhal to a mother who held a boat-share and a father who was \emph{come aboard}, which means his opinion on the boat counted at half and he knew it. She learned the salt pans at twelve and the counting house at fifteen, where she discovered that a contract written on salt-paper in wet weather is no contract at all and that half the port’s fortunes have turned on exactly that. She has the tide-name the dippers gave her at her Salt Walk, which she never uses in Grath because it makes the Grathic people uneasy in a way she enjoys. She came up the Salt Road at fourteen with a consignment of salt and glass for the Greybank factor and stayed three tendays at the Cross because a wet document had turned a debt into nothing and the factor wanted someone to blame who was not himself.

In Sill she met a foundling boy from Low Kiln who could hear a cracked cup through a wall and who had never in his life been offered a contract. She sold him a salt herring for two bits and then, because he had no more coin, sold him the same herring again for a promise to tell her what a cup sounds like when it holds. He told her. She has come back up the Salt Road every year since, sometimes with glass, sometimes with nothing, always with a question about the note, and has never once charged him for the answer. She would say the account is current, which is the only way she has of saying she has not forgotten him.

When they are both grown she will be the person who can take him out of Grath by boat and who will not do it for nothing, because a man who is given a passage in Vhalen is a man who owes a passage, and she has spent her life knowing exactly what that debt is worth. She will also be the one to tell him, in a light Vhaleni voice, that the Sallow has no Hearth of its own and that the dippers have measured the draw and told the Council only what was profitable, which is the first honest answer he will get about a foreign god.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Her wound is the contract. She has grown up in a country with no word for friend, and she has built her whole life on accounts that are current, which means she cannot admit that she wants to see a man without putting a price on it. She fears being in a room where someone says \emph{kindly} and means it, because she has no way to enter it and therefore no way to refuse it. She fears the salt-thing, which follows boats at night and taps the hull where the Warm is stored, and she fears more than that a hard winter that closes the coast pass and leaves her in a country where ash is swept up rather than sold. Her contradiction: the woman who turns damp paper into a fortune has kept one document dry for ten years — a scrap of Grathic slate-rubbing with a cup’s note on it — and calls it \emph{nothing, just a thing I carried}, which is a lie in the precise Vhaleni grammar she uses for the truth.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants a boat-share of her own, which in Vhalen means a share held through her mother and not given by a husband, which is the nearest thing to freedom her country allows. She thinks she wants the boy from the clay country to come to Vhal and hear the Sallow draw, which she has described to him as a sound although it is not one, and which he has never stopped wanting to hear. What she needs is to understand that a person can be loved without being entered, which in Vhalen is a thought so foreign that she will have to learn a Grathic word for it before she can say it, and that the debt between them cannot be settled by a passage on a boat because it is not a debt at all. Their romance can only begin in adulthood, when she holds the share and he is no longer a boy with no coin for a second herring.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the man whose account is current.
