She has sold him the same herring twice, charged him nothing for ten years, and kept a slate-rubbing of a cup’s note in a dry place, which is the most Vhaleni declaration of love ever made. The tension is that he cannot offer her a contract she can accept, because he has no house and no coin, and she cannot offer him a gift she can admit to, because she has no word for one.

\textbf{Veno Brine} — the Greybank factor for whom she first carried glass, a man who can turn a damp document into a fortune and has done so at her expense once, which she has never forgiven and has never refused work from. He has offered to buy her mother’s boat-share twice, which is the offer that will eventually send her back up the Salt Road for good.

\textbf{Neri Sail, chief dipper} — who named her tide and has never once told her what the Sallow draw really is, which she has measured for herself and entered in a private tally that the Council never sees.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — her supplier of eels and the only Grathic person who understands that a contract can be a kindness if both parties know it. They haggle ferociously and have never once short-changed the other, which in two different countries is the same as friendship.

\textbf{Avis Wake} — a Council member who votes against extending the Salt Road’s tax to the glass boats and whom Pia has begun to suspect is voting for her own share rather than anyone’s, which is Vhaleni politics in its purest form.

\textbf{Solene Ember} — the thrice-returned woman she met once in a counting house in Greybank, who remembered three deaths and said them in a warm Sere voice, and whom Pia has never forgotten, because a person who remembers being returned is a person who understands what a contract is worth.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. She enters at the Cross with salt on her sleeves, selling a herring twice to a boy with no coin, and asking him what a cup sounds like when it holds.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 90 (the Cross, the second sale of the same herring).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Solene Ember}{the woman who remembers three deaths}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Solene Ember, Solene, the beautiful return, the woman with three names}\CRfield{Role}{love-interest}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 776, the Bowl of Sere (first life; returned whole in Y.H. 799, 804 and 809)}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Sere; returned three times whole)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Bowl of Sere; a great house that bought poor measures to make one beautiful return}\CRfield{Allegiance}{House Ember of the Ten Bowls; exhibited at feasts; later a counting house in Greybank}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 500}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Thirty-six by her last return and by her first, because a beautiful return brings back the shape the new walls could hold and her house paid to lose as little as possible. She is very beautiful in the way a well-kept thing is beautiful — smooth, calm, without a want she cannot immediately satisfy — and the calm is the burn, which she knows and which she has learned to display as proof of her owner’s generosity. She has three names and remembers which death each one belonged to, and sometimes answers to the wrong one in a fever, which in Sere is considered charming and in the north is considered a Second Want. Her hands are long-fingered and adorned with rings that are not hers, and she removes them one at a time when she is about to say something true, which is a habit her first husband noticed and her second did not. She smells of warm wine, myrrh, ash from a public bowl and, under it, the faint mineral sweetness of a vessel that has been poured three times and holds less each time. The thing people notice first is her calm; the thing they notice last is that she cannot be made to hold a cup steady on the third day after a feast, which is the lead in the glaze and not, as the priests say, the price of beauty.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Long vowels, open Sere courtesy, and a warmth so complete that it reads as honesty and is actually the most polished tool in the empire. She speaks in the plural of houses — \emph{we emptied, we burned the slate, we were returned} — because a single person in Sere is a thing with no standing. She names a death the way another woman might name a birthday, and says \emph{the third one} without irony, because the third one is the one she remembers least and therefore fears most. When she wants to say something she cannot bear she becomes extremely polite, which is the Sere equivalent of a door shutting. She has a habit of asking a person what debt they intend to carry, which is the question asked at the Bowl at sixteen, and asking it of adults in the north as if it were small talk, which it is not.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “I was returned in the year of the green bowl. No — the third one. I was returned in the year of the green bowl and I have had two since. Which one are you asking about?”

\item “We burn the debt-slates by lot at the end of a feast and then we make new ones. You have heard this and thought it was generosity. It is a method.”

\item “A beautiful return is a crack covered with lead. You’ve seen it in your pots. I’m the pot.”

\item “What debt do you intend to carry? I ask everyone. In the north they think it’s a question about money. It isn’t.”

\item “I don’t want to be kept. I want to be held while I’m here and let go before the third day. That’s the whole of it. Ask me on the third day and I’ll say something else.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born in the Bowl of Sere to a household of the Ten Bowls and given seven names, of which she kept the one she liked, which was Solene. At sixteen she emptied the bowl of warm wine at the Bowl feast and named a debt she intended to carry, and the applause was for the debt, not for her, which is the first thing she remembers clearly and the last thing she ever misunderstood. Her house bought poor measures from the north at prices no household could refuse and pressed them into a whole return for a beautiful slave, which is how Sere turns a district of thin people into one decorative person, and she was the decoration. She was returned whole in Y.H. 799, 804 and 809, each time with less of herself than the time before, and each time exhibited at feasts as proof of her owner’s generosity. She remembers all three deaths: the first by the Spending fire, the second by a fall from a balcony during a feast, the third by quiet ash administered by a woman who smiled the whole time and said it was a kindness. The fourth return has not been announced, and she has begun to fear that there will not be one, because a vessel poured four times holds almost nothing of the shape it began with.

In Y.H. 810 she was sent north to a counting house in Greybank as part of a trade in grain and returned slaves, which is the southern empire’s way of opening a market, and she has stayed, nominally attached to a factor’s household and in fact kept in a room with good light where she cannot enjoy the light. She has met the foundling Corin when he was a boy in Sill and later as a man, and has watched him listen to a vessel the way a bowl-surgeon in Sere listens to a graft, which is the one thing in the north that reminds her of home. She will love him as an adult, with restraint and with the full knowledge that she may be returned a fourth time and come back as someone who does not know him, which is the wound underneath all the warmth.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Three deaths and a fourth that may not come. She fears the Weighing, where whole returns are announced in a clean voice, because she has stood in a public bowl and heard her own name spoken by a man who had never met her. She fears the third day after any feast, when the lead in the cup makes her hands unsteady and the priests call it the price of beauty, and she has begun to suspect that the lead is in her glaze and not in her soul. She fears that the fourth return will keep her beauty and take the part that remembers, so that she will sit in a good room and smile at a man she does not know and call it mercy. Her contradiction: the woman who teaches that a vessel was made to be emptied gloriously has spent three returns trying to hold on to a shape nobody in her own country thought worth keeping, and calls it \emph{a debt I intend to carry}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants one death that is not a return, which in Sere is a heresy and in her own mouth is a thing she says in the warmest possible voice so that listeners will think it is a joke. She thinks she wants a whole fourth return, which would keep her beautiful and calm and empty. What she needs is to learn that a debt can be carried without being paid, and that a person returned three times is not less real than a person returned once, which is the same argument Dan will one day make about the world and which she can make about herself with a better grace. Their romance can only begin in adulthood, when she has stopped being exhibited and he has stopped being a boy with an ear, and when both can say the word \emph{crack} in the same sentence without flinching.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the northerner who listens. She has known him as a boy and as a man and has never once asked him to explain the ear, because she recognises a person who has been given a gift with a bill attached. The tension is the fourth return: she loves him in full knowledge that the next pouring may take the part of her that knows him, and he, who has spent his life trying to return people without erasing them, is the one man who cannot promise her a whole one.

\textbf{Veno Brine} — the factor who brought her north and keeps her in a room with good light. He is kind to her in the exact way a creditor is kind, and she has never mistaken it, which is why he keeps her and why he distrusts her.

\textbf{Pia of the Quay} — the Vhaleni salt girl who asked her once, in a counting house, what debt she intended to carry, and did not applaud the answer. Solene has remembered that small act of not applauding longer than most applause.

\textbf{House Ember} — her owners, whose generosity she displays and whose accounts she has read in secret. She knows exactly how many northern measures bought her third return and has entered the number in a place no factor can find, which is a thing she has in common with Alda Reed and Wessen, though they will never meet.

\textbf{Valen Openhand, the Bowl-Priest} — who taught the Spending and is himself one of the fullest vessels in the empire. He has asked her three times whether she is grateful, and she has said yes three times, and the third time she removed a ring before she said it, which he noticed and did not mention.

\textbf{Marra} — the collector whose slate recorded one of the returns that bought a beautiful person. Solene has seen the slate-name in a Court document in Greybank and has begun to wonder whether the collector’s fingers went grey paying for someone else’s display, which is a question she will one day ask and regret asking.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. She enters in a counting house in Greybank, removing a ring one at a time while she tells a boy which death she means, and the reader will think her a curiosity in a southern trader’s household until the third day after a feast, when the cup betrays her.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 500 (Greybank counting house, the three deaths).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Hald Iron}{the master of the floor who reads a paper he will not use}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Hald Iron, the master of the floor, Hald of the Tenth Clamp, Iron}\CRfield{Role}{mentor}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 758, Keld Keep, the Rim}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Keldish)}\CRfield{Origin}{Keld Keep, the First Seam, the Rim}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Order of the Tenth Clamp; master of the duelling floor; keeper of Haldre’s surviving copy}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 190}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Fifty-four, short and built sideways, with the heavy shoulders of a man who has spent thirty years bracing against a stone face. His measure-number is tattooed on his left forearm in Keldish numerals and has been cut out and replaced twice, because a mastery that carries more than it did must be entered again; around the old numbers the skin is pale and puckered, so that the arm reads like a ledger with corrections. His right palm is a flat shiny graft from a trial of hot iron he lost at thirty and has never explained. He wears a clamp at his belt, not as a weapon but as a tool a smith carries, and he cleans it while he thinks. He smells of hot iron, lead dust, coal smoke and the metallic sweat of the deep seam. The thing people notice first is the palm; the thing they notice last is that when he is genuinely interested in a man, he states that man’s measure aloud, which in Keld is a kind of attention and in the lowlands is an insult.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Clipped, numerical, with the number before the noun — \emph{forty of head, not capacity; two fingers, gone; one way, open}. He states a measure before a name and expects the same courtesy. He never uses a word where a figure will do and has a habit of counting aloud under his breath while he watches a person move, which unnerves lowlanders and comforts Keldish men, who know he is reading their ways. He does not explain a procedure twice. When he is pleased he says \emph{that will hold}, which is the highest praise the floor knows and sounds like a judgement on a pot.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Number first. Then the name. Everyone in Keld knows this. The lowlands are full of men who won’t learn it and are surprised when they’re checked.”

\item “You want the first way. I can cut it. I can’t make you want it afterwards. That’s the part nobody teaches and everybody pays.”

\item “Forty-two. No. Forty and a half. You’re carrying more than you think, and it’s showing at the wrist.”

\item “The paper is read once a year. It is not used. There’s a difference, and the Order has lived on that difference for a hundred years.”

\item “That will hold. Don’t make me say it twice. I won’t.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born in Keld Keep and went down the First Seam at thirteen for his Descent, where his number changed from thirty-one to thirty-eight and he was given the keys instead of the pick, which the Keldish consider a kindness and is not one. He became a duellist of the Order of the Tenth Clamp, won the floor at twenty-nine, and lost the use of his right palm in a trial of hot iron at thirty, after which he had it grafted flat and never held a clamp in that hand again. He has taught the first way to four hundred young Keldish men and has watched a third of them open a way they could not close and go cold in a season. He has never taught a lowlander, because the Order’s method is the only thing Keld owns that the Court cannot tax.

In Y.H. 710 he was given one of the nine copies of Haldre’s suppressed paper to burn. He burned eight. He has kept his copy in a tin lined with warm-stone in the First Seam and has read it on the Last Day of every year since, which is fifty-two readings, and has never once agreed with it in a way that would make him act. In Y.H. 806 he was shown the crossed-out survey entry of Y.H. 588 — a returned man with a branching red mark on his forearm and ribs — and he copied the shape of the mark onto the back of his own tally, where it sits under the numbers. He has never shown it to a Vorr, who would only say that the numbers were correct.

When a lowlander boy with an unusual ear arrives at the floor he will be measured before he is welcomed, and Hald will find something in the boy’s ways that he does not have a number for, which will interest him more than anything in thirty years. He will offer to cut the first way and will name the price honestly: a fortnight of uselessness, a permanent mark, and the understanding that a way once cut is never closed. He will not tell the boy the other price, which is that the Order has kept Haldre’s paper for a century precisely so that it never has to be used.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
The palm. And the thirty-four young men whose names he keeps in a tally he does not show, because a man who teaches the first way is responsible for what opens afterwards. He fears the Sweating, which he has survived twice and smelled a hundred times in the hot-lead warning; he fears that the Order’s secrecy has made it a museum instead of a guild; and he fears the boy with the mark, because a mender’s failure is a thing the Keldish call a birth-flower and consider unlucky, and Hald has spent fifty years deciding that he does not believe in luck. His contradiction: the man who would not burn the paper has built his life on not using it, and calls that prudence, when it is the same refusal Garl Vail made with a different tool.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the method to stay in Keldish hands and the floor to keep its men alive, which are sometimes the same want and often not. He thinks he wants the boy to fail, so that the Order’s caution is vindicated. What he needs is to teach a man who will use the way for something other than a duel — who will cut a way to stop a leak rather than to push a man off a ledge — and to learn that a method kept unused for a hundred years is not knowledge but a debt.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Rusk of the Weir} — offered a place on the floor thirty years ago and refused it. Hald has never forgiven the refusal and has never stopped being glad of it, because a man who asks questions in that voice would be dangerous with a first way. They have not spoken since.

\textbf{Siv of the First Seam} — his surveyor and the keeper of the crossed-out page. Hald respects the man’s patience and distrusts what he finds in the stone, which is the proper Keldish attitude toward the deep records. Siv has shown him the mark once; Hald has copied it; neither has said what they think it means.

\textbf{Corin} — the lowlander with the ear. Hald has decided he is a student and a threat in the same person, which is how the floor has always classified its best men. He will be fair to him, which in Keld is a form of respect, and will not protect him from the price of the way.

\textbf{Karric VII, Warden of the Rim} — the ruler who keeps the true-Hearth claim alive for reasons both men know are commercial. Hald serves him and has never believed him, which is another difference that has kept Keld independent.

\textbf{Haldre} — whose paper he reads annually and whose ending he intends never to bring about. Hald has begun, in the last ten years, to wonder whether refusing to use a thing is the same as refusing to believe it, and has not entered the question in any tally.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters on the duelling floor, cleaning a clamp while he states a lowlander’s measure aloud, and the reader will understand from the correction in his tattoo that he has been paying for his own knowledge for thirty years.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 190 (the First Seam, the offer of the first way).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Rusk of the Weir}{the man who asked a question and answered it badly}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Rusk, Rusk of the Weir, the old man above the Fishstairs, the teacher with the locked drawer}\CRfield{Role}{mentor}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 762, the Weir, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Weir, one league upriver of Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Unbanked; a room above the Fishstairs; the eel traps he walks at dusk}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 70}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Seventy and thin as a stripped willow, with hands that have spent fifty years in cold water and have the pale, dangling look of things pulled from a weir. He wears a coat lined with ice-mouse fur, which is a joke the collectors make and which he has never acknowledged, and a hat whose brim is stiff with old salt. One eye waters in the wind; he says it is the river, and it is not. He walks with a sideways hitch from a winter he spent on the plank walk when the Weir froze and he would not come in. He smells of wet rope, cold fish, tallow smoke and, in his room, of the iron he keeps in a drawer. The thing people notice first is that he never sits down indoors; the thing they notice last is the drawer, which has a small lock and which he touches with his knuckles when a certain kind of question is asked. He has a habit of turning a question back on the asker before answering, so that the answer, when it comes, sounds like a correction of somebody else’s mistake.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
He speaks in questions, and answers his own questions badly. He never says what he wants. He asks what \emph{you} want, then corrects the answer until it resembles what he decided you should have wanted all along. He is exact about vessels and vague about himself, and in this he is the mirror image of Marra, who is exact about other people’s warmth and vague about her own in the opposite way. His questions are not socratic; they are a hand feeling along a wall for a switch. When he is frightened he becomes gentle, which is the most alarming thing about him.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “What do you hear? No. Listen again. What do you hear now that you’ve stopped trying to be clever?”

\item “You want to know what’s in the drawer. Everybody wants that. Nobody wants what it costs to open.”

\item “I taught you to listen. Did I teach you what to do with what you heard? No. That’s the part I kept.”

\item “Who told you a clamp was mercy? I did. Was I wrong? Ask me again in twenty years.”

\item “Don’t thank me. Thank the boy you’ll be when you’ve decided whether to say \emph{leave it} out loud. I’m only the old man who held the iron.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born at the Weir and has lived within a league of it all his life, which in Grath is not narrowness but a kind of depth. He kept the traps for thirty years, sold eels at the Fishstairs, and was known as a man who could tell a collector’s boat by its oars before it came round the bend and who would not say what he had heard through the ice. He learned to read a vessel the way a trapper reads a weir: by where the water slows, by what has been chewed, by the note a thing makes when tapped. He has no name for the ranks and does not use them. He calls the full note \emph{the note that holds}, and a crack \emph{a place that has been used}.

In Y.H. 675 a man named in a Court file as a person of the sealed opinion came to the Weir and asked Rusk whether he had ever noticed that a man’s ways run in spirals. Rusk said he had. The man asked what Rusk wanted to do about it. Rusk asked what the man wanted. Neither answered. Three years later Rusk opened the drawer for the first time and found a copy of a paper that had been read by nine people in Y.H. 702 and suppressed in 710. He has read it once a year since, on the Last Day, when the ash in the corners is not swept.

He saw the foundling Corin at the Fishstairs when the boy was small, turning his head toward a pot before anyone tapped it. He began to teach him not because the boy was gifted but because the boy was willing to decide: he had once watched a drowned eel-trapper be left where he lay and had not cried or prayed, only looked. Rusk recognised the look and has spent the years since shaping it into an instrument. He has never told the boy why he teaches him. He has never once asked where his memory came from, because he already knows.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
A life spent in cold water has left his hands almost without feeling, which means he can no longer find the full note by touch and must hear it instead — a humiliating reversal for a man whose whole craft was the knuckle. He fears the Weighing, where whole returns are announced in a clean voice, because he has paid for one and knows what the payment was. He fears that he has taught a boy to decide without teaching him what a decision costs when the person deciding is the instrument. He fears Marra, who is the only person who has ever asked him directly whether he still believes in the Final Banking, and who does not wait for the answer. His contradiction is the whole of him: the teacher who warns against every clamp has kept the key to one in a drawer for thirty years, and calls it \emph{not having decided yet}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the sealed world — a vessel that leaks nothing, owes nothing, and can give nothing away, which he has called paradise for thirty years and has never once described in plain words to a living soul. He thinks he wants a mender cold enough to do the sealing. What he needs is to understand that the boy he is shaping is not a tool but a man with a dead daughter on another planet, and that the willingness to say \emph{leave it} is not the same as the willingness to let the world stop wanting. When he finally opens the drawer for Corin it will be too late to be a lesson and just in time to be a betrayal.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — his student, his instrument, the only person he has ever taught without being asked. He loves the boy in the only dialect available to him: a question, a correction, a hand on the wrist moved aside at exactly the right moment. The tension is that every lesson is a lie by omission. Rusk knows where the boy came from and what paid for him, and teaches him anyway, so that one day the boy will be able to look at a dying world and say what Rusk has never been able to say about his own life.

\textbf{Marra} — the collector who wrote the name before the face. They are the two halves of the same purchase: she pays with her fingers, he pays with his silence. They have not spoken plainly in twelve years. She has begun to go grey and he has begun to be gentle, and each knows the other is nearer the end of the work than either will admit.

\textbf{Orra Weir} — he walks her traps at dusk and has never once asked permission, which in a Weir household is a declaration of something. She keeps forty-one knots and he keeps a drawer, and both have decided the other is keeping the wrong count.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — they were in the room on Stone 7. She crossed herself at the birth-flower; he looked at the collector’s slate. Neither has said a word about it to the other in three years, which is how neighbours in a small district show the deepest possible respect.

\textbf{Sella Vail} — he has never entered her house and has never explained why. She has never asked. They have an understanding built out of not meeting: he teaches her foundling to listen, she lets him, and both pretend this is a coincidence of the Weir and the Clay Yard.

\textbf{Hald Iron} — the Keldish clamp-master who once offered Rusk a place on the duelling floor and was refused. Rusk says he refused because of the cold. Hald says he refused because he was afraid of what he would learn there. Both are right.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters as an old man at the Fishstairs who asks a boy what he heard and then tells him he heard it wrong, and the reader will take him for a kind teacher for a very long time.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 70 (the Fishstairs, the first question).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Wessen}{the man who taught eleven words for a deduction and none for sorry}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Provost Wessen, the provost, the man who removed the verbs}\CRfield{Role}{mentor}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 761, the Hearthseat, on the Keel}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; officer of the Keeping)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Hearthseat; slate-name entered on the rolls, no house and no kin}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the College of Assessors, Slate Row, the Hearthseat; keeper of the lawful deduction}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 420}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Fifty-one and dry as a filed quill, with the narrow, bloodless hands of a man who has written more lives than he has ever held. He wears the College’s black wool with a strip of white at the cuff to show that he teaches rather than practises, and he keeps his nails pared to the quick because ink under a nail is a thing that can be read. His slate is always ruled into columns and his chalk is always new; he has never been seen with a blunt instrument of any kind, which his students take as a warning about the kind of accuracy he expects. He has a habit of turning a slate face-down before he speaks to a person, so that the person understands they are not being entered, and another habit of turning it face-up again when he has finished, so that they understand they were. He smells of slate dust, dry paper and the cold stone of the College, which is the same smell as a room nobody has warmed in order to save money.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Ledgerhand as a teacher speaks it: the verbs removed, the person removed, the sentence left standing like a frame with the picture taken out. He never says \emph{please} and never says \emph{sorry}, and has taught that the two words are the same absence wearing different coats. He corrects students by their slate-names, which in the College means they do not have birth-names to be corrected by, and the correction lands somewhere colder than Ostrowski’s full name ever did. When he wants to say something kind he gives a figure, because a figure can be checked. When he wants to say something cruel he says \emph{the account stands} and lets the student supply the person.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Eleven words for a deduction. One of them is correct. The other ten are for people who want to feel something while they write.”

\item “You have written a sentence with a verb in it. Who is it that did this thing? In this college, nobody. Write it again.”

\item “Marchetti — I beg your pardon. Rennick. Your hand is a good hand. That is not praise. It is a measurement.”

\item “The printed figure is one in twelve. The true figure is taught in this room to people who will never print it. That is the whole of your education.”

\item “You want to know what a life was worth. That is a question with a verb in it. Sit down.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was entered on the rolls at nineteen and rose through the College by a route he describes as \emph{steady correction}, which means he has never made a public mistake and has made a great many private ones. He has taught Ledgerhand for twenty-six years and has had four hundred students, of whom three hundred and ninety-one are officers of the Keeping and nine are names he will not say aloud, because they were removed from the rolls and their slates are kept in a second file that he maintains himself. He believes the College’s purpose is not to value lives but to decide who is allowed to value them, which he considers a distinction that protects the world from arithmetic done by people with a stake in the answer.

He knows the printed figure is false. He teaches the true method to his own students and prints the false one for everyone else, and has done this every year since Y.H. 790, when a Greybank slate with a line that did not balance was corrected in a second hand and filed without comment. He has never asked whose second hand it was, because an assessor who asks about a correction is an assessor who has begun to add, and adding is the first step toward a thing the College has no word for. In Y.H. 803 he was shown the slate again, with the name \emph{Marra} in the first hand, and he entered nothing about it in any report, which he has entered in his private tally as the only honest thing he has ever written.

When a foundling from Low Kiln arrives in the Hearthseat with a clinical ear and an inability to read a ledger, Wessen will teach him to read it for a price: his own name on a slate, entered in Ledgerhand, which is the same as having no name at all, and one memory burned thin in the Weighing Hall to pay for the lesson. He will not tell him in advance which memory. He will say only that the account will be opened and that the boy should decide whether he can bear to be entered.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He removed the verbs from his own speech so successfully that he can no longer say a thing happened, which means he can no longer say that anything was done to anyone. His wound is a language with no actor in it. He fears the Vorr, who recognise the numbers in the oldest slates and will not translate them, because a people who can read the Deep Hand and refuse to is a people who have already judged the Keeping and found it heavy. He fears anyone who can add, because adding a district’s deduction to a district’s warmth produces a number the College has spent eight hundred years not printing. His contradiction: the man who teaches that a life has no actor has kept a private tally of his own acts for thirty years, and calls it \emph{a record of corrections}, which is the most human thing in the College and the one thing he would never enter in a report.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the method to remain in the College alone and the valuation of lives to be a guild secret rather than a public argument. He thinks he wants accuracy. What he needs is to learn that a deduction with no actor in the sentence is still a deduction taken from a person, and that the passive voice is not a protection but a tool, like the clamp and the cold iron tube. When Dan reads a Sarnish ledger for the first time and understands the one-in-three, it will be Wessen’s teaching that lets him do it, and Wessen’s voice he will hear in the sentence he hates most.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Rennick} — his best student, a young assessor with a gift for addition that Wessen has tried three times to correct out of him. He has not succeeded and has not reported the failure, which is a form of protection in the College and the only kind he can manage.

\textbf{Marra} — an officer whose reports are the most exact in his service and whose slate once failed to balance in his own hand. He has never asked her why and has stopped correcting her work, which she has noticed and entered nowhere.

\textbf{Steward Derren} — the head of the Keeping, who has praised the College’s accuracy twice in twenty years. Wessen has decided that praise from that man is a warning, and has begun to keep the second file in a more secure place.

\textbf{Ludo of the Old Kiln} — a Grathic scholar who found a slate in the ash throat and has begun asking the College questions in the Deep Hand’s shape. Wessen has not answered him and has not had him removed, which is the precise boundary of his courage.

\textbf{Corin} — the lowlander with the ear who cannot add and can hear a cracked vessel through a wall. Wessen has decided to teach him because the boy’s deficiencies are interesting: a man who can read a wall but not a ledger is a man who will one day ask why the two disagree.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — a hearth-maid who was paid by the Court to keep the birth-slates and has kept a second set instead. Wessen knows the second set exists in the abstract, the way a man knows a drawer is locked, and has never ordered it searched.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters in a counting room in Slate Row, turning a slate face-down before he speaks to a boy, which the boy will not understand until he learns to read what is on it.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 420 (the College of Assessors, the Hearthseat).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Alda Reed}{the hearth-maid who crossed herself and told nobody}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Alda, Alda the hearth-maid, the keeper of the second notes}\CRfield{Role}{ally}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 770, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; House Reed}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Hearth-Maids; birth-slates of Low Kiln; collector’s assistant at Sill}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 1}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Forty-two and built like a woman who has spent her life lifting other people’s children: broad shoulders, a permanent stoop from leaning over births, and hands so steady that a mother will grab her wrist rather than her arm when the pain comes. Her nails are scrubbed to the quick and still have clay under them. She wears a grey wool shawl in every season and a little tin box on a cord inside her bodice, which she touches when a question is asked and never opens in company. She smells of soap, warm milk, tallow and the faint mineral tang of the Ash she has promised herself she will sweep tomorrow. The thing people notice first is her calm; the thing they notice last is the crossed sign she makes with two fingers at the corner of a birth-flower, which she does not know she does in front of strangers.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Practical instruction in proverbs and half-sentences. She addresses the newborn, the stone, the slip, the mother, and only then the father, in that order. She does not say \emph{please} and she does not say \emph{sorry}, and she does not consider this coldness; she considers it the correct amount of words for a room where somebody is in pain. She has a habit of answering a question with the date. When she is lying she becomes unusually exact.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Warm the stone first. A cold stone is a stone, not a kindness.”

\item “Don’t set that near the fire. You’ll pay for it in the thaw.”

\item “He grew worse on the third day. So did the one before him. That’s all I’ve written.”

\item “The Court struck the name out. They didn’t strike the child out.”

\item “I crossed myself. It’s what my mother did. Don’t make it more than that.”

\item “In the levy. Among the children taken for the account this month.” --- the lie, delivered as an entry and never as a story.

\item “A woman with a thin return goes asking after the levy and a hand comes down on a slate that was better left where it lay.” --- mercy spoken in the grammar of a warning.

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born in Low Kiln to a family of reed-cutters and became a hearth-maid at nineteen, which in Grath means midwife, keeper of the birth-slate, and the person a household calls before it calls a collector. She has attended something over four hundred births. The first of the second notes was not hers. In Y.H. 651, long before she was born, a hearth-maid in Low Kiln attended an infant who grew worse on the third day and spoke words in a language no one in the district knew. The Court’s physician at Greybank wrote it down as a settling of the graft, struck the phrase \emph{Second Want} from his own report, and paid the maid for her trouble. That note passed down the order, from hand to hand and tin to tin, until it came to Alda. She has added to it every year since she took it up: every infant who grew worse on the third day, every word that did not belong to Grathic, every birth-flower shaped like a branch instead of a rose. The notes live under a floorboard in her house, in a second hand that the Court has never seen. Alda Reed’s is the fullest set in the order.

She attended Enna Vail’s death in Y.H. 804 and sat with Sella through a night in which nothing she did mattered. She attended Corin’s birth on Stone 7, Y.H. 812, crossed herself at the branching red flower across his ribs, and told nobody what she had seen. Three days later she told Dessa Silt that her child had gone in the levy, which was a lie told to keep a whole-return infant off a slate that every officer in Slate Row would have wanted to read. She has not slept properly since.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Four hundred births and one she cannot account for. On Day 1 the sign is made in front of witnesses and covered with a word and a date nobody asked for; from that hour she is a woman who has hidden a thing in a room where everybody is arranging silence. She fears collectors — not their tubes, which she has held, but their slates, which are always in a better hand than hers. She fears that the second notes are a treasonous thing and that keeping them has made her a person who waits for children to get worse. She fears Dessa’s face in the street. Her contradiction: the woman who insists that everything be written down within three days has spent thirty years keeping a second record that cannot be shown to anyone, and calls it \emph{not wanting trouble}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants one of the Court’s physicians to read her notes and say the words she struck out herself. She thinks she wants the boy she delivered to grow up ordinary, so that she can stop looking at him. What she needs is to understand that she has been a keeper of the truth in the only way available to a hearth-maid: badly, in secret, and at a cost to the woman she lied to.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the child she delivered and crossed herself over. She has watched him for three years without once saying what she saw, and has begun to suspect he knows that she knows. \emph{Ch 1:} she lifted him, cleaned him in a deliberate order (face, neck, groin, knees, cord), pressed the wool flat with the same two fingers that had made the sign, and would not say his name. \emph{Ch 2:} she washed him in the same order on the second morning, reached for the slate first and pushed the writing further under, touched the tin at her bodice, and made the crossed sign again at the corner of the flower without knowing she was doing it in front of him. She said warm and breathing was all she had set down, which was not true. \emph{Ch 3:} at the fringe of the market she kept the birth-slates in a row, turning the writing down when a mother came near. She stopped at the basket to straighten the bottom slate, touched the tin inside her bodice a third time, saw the red branching edge of the flower at his wrist where the wool had ridden back, and crossed two fingers at the corner of it before she knew she had done it in front of a market full of people. She covered the sign with a date nobody had asked for: \emph{Stone seven; the account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out.}

\textbf{Sella Vail} — they have traded a silence since the boy was three days old. Sella has never asked what Alda wrote; Alda has never asked why Sella took the child. This is not friendship. It is an arrangement with a door in it that both women have agreed not to open. \emph{Ch 1:} the arrangement begins in the birth room, before a word is spoken between them: Alda lays the slate flat as the hand in the door goes back out. \emph{Ch 2:} when Sella came for the boy she put her hand near the slate on the bench and did not touch it, and neither woman looked at the other. The arrangement holds. \emph{Ch 3:} at the market’s edge the two women are a pace apart without a word passing: Sella’s little-door hand closes the wool over the red edge of the flower, and a few paces away the collector’s clerk’s hand closes on his slate. Both are careful hands entering the same child in two different accounts, and neither account has been shown to him.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — she lied to this woman about her own child and has taken her barley money from Sella’s house ever since. When Dessa speaks to her in the street, Alda answers with the weather and hates the sound of her own voice. \emph{Ch 1:} she delivered Dessa’s child and met the unasked question with dates and weather, her hand on the slate rather than on the mother. \emph{Ch 2:} she stood behind Dessa in the Clay Yard as the bundle went up the hill and told her the boy had gone in the levy among the children taken for the account; she became exact about dates nobody had asked for, and the figure was never spoken. The lie is told to keep a slate unread and has begun to curdle.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — they work the same room standing and trade small facts at the door. \emph{Ch 1:} neither sat where the slate lay open, and each kept her face arranged for the other. \emph{Ch 2:} Mira is not in the room; her crust of brown-edged barley is the last warmth that crossed it from her hands.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — he knows she wrote something down the night Enna died and has never asked to see it. She has begun to be afraid of the day he does, because the boy in his house is the one thing Jorra could be persuaded to burn for.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — pays her to keep the birth-slates and has never asked about the second notes. She has decided this is because he knows and does not want a copy of anything, which may be true.

\textbf{Orra Weir} — was in the room on Stone 7 and tied a knot she cannot account for. They never discuss it. Orra is the only person Alda has ever let near the subject of the third day, and even she has not seen the tin.

\textbf{Ch 4.} The arrangement with Sella held in the death-house by a half-pace and a slate turned writing-down: she wrote what she did not leave lying about and Sella did not ask. With the neighbour mother she gave dates where comfort was asked for and a graft settling where an answer was wanted, and the woman wrapped the feet. With Dessa there was no meeting at all this chapter; the lie is three days old and already a fact in the woman’s face. She touched the tin twice and crossed the sign twice more without knowing she did it in front of a room and a basket.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
The two gestures that will define her are on the page in Chapter 1: the crossed sign she does not know she makes in front of strangers, and the exact, unasked-for dates with which she covers it. In Chapter 2 both gestures repeat and a third is added. She reaches for the slate before the child again, touches the little tin on its cord twice more, crosses the sign over the flower in front of the boy without knowing it, and answers every unasked question with an exact history --- a cooper’s seventh in Mud End, the account going up on the eighth before the paper went out --- until the dates arrive faster than dates have any business arriving. Then she tells Dessa the boy went in the levy. The lie is told as mercy and cannot be undone. In Chapter 3 she keeps the birth-slates at the edge of the market, touches the tin a third time and makes the sign a third time in front of witnesses, and the date she uses to cover it is the same one she gave Dessa: the account up on the eighth, before the paper went out. The market goes on around the sign and the date and the red edge at a wrist, arranging itself around the things it does not read. The tin under the floorboard is years away from being read.

On Days 5 and 6 the tin is touched twice more and the second notes acquire a second hiding place. She comes under the Vail roof to look at the child she delivered, reaches for the slate before him, presses the fold at his shoulder too long, crosses the sign at the red edge of his wrist without knowing she does it in front of him, and answers a mother’s three questions with exact dates and a graft settling. In the death-house she sets her own slate writing-down on the bench and then writes a second note that she turns down and slides under a milk cup, where no mother and no officer will think to pick it up. She crosses her fingers again at a red mark at a rekindled child’s hairline without knowing it. The Court’s struck word is still not spoken in the room; the kindest voice in it is closing a question nobody opened, and the boy knows the shape of the gesture and has no sentence for it.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Delivered Corin in the front room of House Silt and worked with steady hands, clay under her nails. Reached for the slate before the child. Put her two fingers to the branching flower, noticed she had done it before witnesses, and covered the act with \emph{birth-flower} plus an unasked-for history from Nine Pots: Y.H. 806, fourth day of Stone, a potter’s third child, now a carter in Stonemouth. Touched the tin at her bodice twice. Gave a date and a weather report for the unasked question and said warm and breathing was all she had set down. Closed the slate flat on the bench while Sella Vail’s hand hovered in the door.

\item Ch 2: On Day 2 washed the child in her order, reached for the slate first, touched the tin a further time, made the crossed sign over the flower before witnesses without knowing it, and gave a date and weather where comfort was expected. On Day 3 stood behind Dessa at the end of the Clay Yard and told her the boy was entered in the levy among the children taken for the account that month, that the account went up on the eighth before the paper went out, and that a woman with a thin return who goes asking draws a hand down on a slate better left where it lay. Said warm and breathing was all she had set down. The number was never spoken; she went on giving dates after the question had stopped.

\item Ch 3: Day 4, Account-day. Kept the birth-slates under her arm and in a row on a bench at the fringe of the market, turning the writing down when a mother came near. Touched the tin inside her bodice once, the third sighting of it, when there was no question in the air to answer. Saw the edge of the birth-flower at the boy’s wrist, crossed two fingers at the corner of it without knowing she had done it in front of a market full of people, and covered the sign with an exact, unasked-for date: \emph{Stone seven; the account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out.} Nobody commented on the sign or the date or the red edge; the two women bargaining over short weight went on standing side by side without making their figures agree.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Days 5 and 6. Came under the Vail roof on Stone 11 to see the child she delivered, reached for the slate before him, pressed the fold flat at his shoulder longer than a fold required, touched the tin inside her bodice, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the flower at his wrist without knowing he saw it, and gave exact dates where the neighbour mother asked for comfort: \emph{Stone seven; the account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out.} Called the rekindled child’s second chill a graft settling and wrote on her own slate what she then turned writing-down on the bench. On Stone 12, in the death-house, touched the tin again, wrote a second note, turned it down and slid it under a milk cup, crossed the sign at a red mark at the child’s hairline, and went on giving dates after the questions had stopped. Neither note was read and the tin was not opened.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Orra Weir}{the woman who keeps the traps and counts the names}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Orra, Orra of the Weir, the trap-keeper}\CRfield{Role}{ally}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 778, the Weir, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Weir, one league upriver of Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the eel traps at the Weir; the Fish Blessing; House Weir}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 12}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Thirty-four, with hands like old rope and a grey streak through black hair from a winter she spent on the weir when she was twenty and would do again. Eel scars lie across both forearms in pale arcs, and she never covers them, because a trap-keeper without scars is a trap-keeper who does not work. She smells of river silt, cold fish and tallow smoke, and in the spring of wet mud so strongly that people at the Cross know she has come before they see her. She keeps her accounts in knots on a cord of grey wool and will not sit at a table where a slate is open; she learned the habit from a Fen woman who sold her reed and taught her that a written name is a name somebody else can read. The thing people notice first is the grey streak; the thing they notice last is the cord, which she touches when a number is spoken.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Slow, and when she is tired the nasal half-song of the Fen comes into it, which she has never entirely lost. She tells a story sideways and gives a date where another person would give an opinion. She never answers a question about the levy directly, but she will tell you which night the collector’s boat came and how many oars it had. When she is lying she ties a knot, which is invisible to anyone who does not know the cord.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “I don’t keep names on slate. Slates burn. Knots don’t.”

\item “Forty. I tied forty knots in the year of the flower. I tied one more and I don’t know what to call it.”

\item “The river takes what the Court doesn’t. You can shout at either one. I’ve tried.”

\item “A trap works because the eel can’t see the way out. That’s all a trap is. Don’t make it more than that.”

\item “You’re the Vail boy. You’ve got her eyes and somebody else’s listening.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She was born at the Weir, one league upriver of Sill, where the eel traps stand on a plank walk and the Fish Blessing is held every Reed 15. She has kept the traps since she was eighteen, knows the river’s moods better than the weather, and can tell a collector’s boat by its oars before it comes round the bend. She married a man who went downriver with a cart of pots and never came back, and has not looked for him, which she calls \emph{not being a fool about it}. Her son Kess was one of the forty children of Low Kiln banked in the first weeks of Stone, Y.H. 812. No return has been announced for him. She tied forty knots that year and then tied a forty-first, because on the night the children were taken she saw Sella Vail carry a covered bundle up the Clay Yard, and the bundle was small and warm and had a flower on it, and she could not account for it.

She was in the room on Stone 7 when the child with the branching red flower was born, and she saw the collector’s slate that night, and saw a name written on it before anyone had seen the infant’s face. She cannot spell the name; she remembers its shape, which is a thing no Court clerk can take away.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
A son spent for a return that never came. She has gone to the Bone Orchard twice looking for a shallow grave with his name on it and found none, which is worse than finding one, because it means his warmth was banked and not wasted and is somewhere in an account she will never see. She fears the Weighing, where whole returns are announced in a clean voice to people whose children are not coming back. She fears that the forty-first knot is a thing she has made into a mystery because a plain answer would be too ordinary: the boy she saw carried was simply another child, and she has needed him not to be. Her contradiction: she sells eels to the Court’s clerk and hates the Court, and has learned the collector’s schedule in order to avoid him and also in order to be there when he comes.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants her son returned whole, which she knows will not happen, and she wants somebody else to say his name who is not herself. She thinks she wants to know what happened to the boy in the covered bundle. What she needs is to understand that a mother’s account is never settled by a return, whole or thin, and that the knot cord is not a ledger but a way of continuing to hold on to something the Court has already booked.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Kess} — her son, one of the Forty. She has a knot for him and no return, and has begun to speak to the knot in the evening, which she knows is not sensible and does not stop.

\textbf{Corin} — the Vail boy. She sees something in him that belongs to the year of the flower and cannot place it: the way he turns his head, the mark under his sleeve, the attention of a person much older than his body. She has never said this to anyone except the cord.

\textbf{Sella Vail} — she blames Sella for taking a child at the very moment forty others were taken, and has never said why she blames her, which leaves the blame floating free and available for anything.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — they were both in the room on Stone 7. Dessa believes her baby died; Orra knows she is lying and has not said. It is the one thing she has never sold at the Fishstairs.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — the only person with whom she has ever discussed the third day. They do not discuss the birth-flower. They discuss the weather and the river and occasionally the number forty, and then one of them touches something and the subject changes.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — her eel buyer, her friend, and the one person she lets near the knots. Mira wants the secret out; Orra wants it kept, which is the only disagreement in a friendship of fifteen years.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — bought her eels for the levy feast and paid two marks short. She has never forgiven the arithmetic, and has served him the same short measure ever since.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. She enters at the Fish Blessing with a cord of knots in her hand, tying a forty-first while the hearth-maids bless the traps and a boy she does not know stands three paces away with his head tilted, listening to the water.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 12 (the Fish Blessing at the Weir, Ilmen 15, Y.H. 813).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Siv of the First Seam}{the man who kept the page that was crossed out}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Siv of the First Seam, Siv, the surveyor, the hunter of the deep seam}\CRfield{Role}{ally}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 770, Keld Keep, the Rim}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Keldish)}\CRfield{Origin}{Keld Keep, the First Seam, the Rim}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Keldish survey of the rim-seam; the Order of the Tenth Clamp; hunter of seam-serpents}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 200}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Forty-two, with the stillness of a man who has spent twenty years sitting in a shaft listening to stone, which makes him seem slower than he is and much harder to interrupt. He is tall for Keldish and slightly stooped from the low roof of the survey, with a thin grey scar across the bridge of his nose from a Warm-flood he did not get out of fast enough, and eyes that have adjusted to dark seams and therefore look vaguely at people in daylight. His measure-number is tattooed on his forearm in hard Keldish numerals and has never been cut out, which in the First Seam is a statement of confidence or stubbornness. He carries a bone tally on a cord and makes marks on it with a nail when a number changes, and he does not carry a clamp, which is unusual for a man of the Order and is the first thing Hald Iron notices about him. He smells of cold stone, hot lead, coal smoke and the particular mineral sweetness that comes just before the Sweating. The thing people notice first is the scar; the thing they notice last is that he listens to walls before he listens to the person standing next to one.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Slow, clipped, numerical, with the Keldish habit of putting the number before the noun — \emph{two seams, one passing, no name for it}. He gives dates where another man would give a judgement and has never in his life offered an opinion about a thing he has not measured. When the Vorr say \emph{the numbers are correct}, he writes it down and does not ask them to expand, because he has learned that a one-sentence answer from a Vorr is a complete answer and that asking for more is a way of losing the number. He is the only Keldish man Hald Iron will allow near the crossed-out file, which is a compliment neither man has ever spoken. When he is frightened he starts counting under his breath, which sounds like the seam itself.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Five repairs in this wall. Four of stone. One of something the translation says is Ash and the Court’s copy says is language. Both translations are guesses. I have entered them both.”

\item “The serpent passed at the third hour. The numbers changed by two. I did not see it whole. Nobody does.”

\item “Y.H. 588. A man with a mark. The entry is crossed out because a surveyor wrote the shape of it and then thought better of it. I have kept the shape. I do not know what it means. That is not the same as not keeping it.”

\item “You ask what holds the world together. I can tell you how many repairs I have counted. I cannot tell you what they were made of. Nobody can. The Vorr will only say the numbers are correct.”

\item “I don’t hunt the thing because it’s a monster. It drinks through the stone where a man can’t reach. So I reach where it does. That’s the whole of it.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He went down the First Seam at thirteen, as every Keldish child does, and his number changed from thirty-four to forty-one on the day of his Descent, which is unusual and made the mine-reeve look at him twice. He became a surveyor of the rim-seam, a hunter of seam-serpents when a passing was measured in the deep shafts, and a man who has spent twenty years entering the repairs of the world in a bone tally that no officer below the rank of assessor is allowed to read. He has survived two Sweatings by being further down than the men who died and by knowing, from the smell of hot lead, that there were minutes and not hours. He has sat with Vorr at the Listening Stones for a season and come away with three sentences, all of them \emph{the numbers are correct}, and has entered all three.

In Y.H. 806 the First Seam survey was sealed and the crossed-out entry of Y.H. 588 was copied into the sealed file at the Hearthseat. Siv made the copy. He is the clerk who could not have known why he was asked, and he has known ever since. He copied the shape of the branching red mark onto the back of his own tally, under the numbers, and has carried it for six years without showing it to anyone except Hald Iron, who copied it onto the back of his own and has not spoken of it since. In Y.H. 811 he recorded a repair in the rim-seam as \emph{words that hold} and was not removed from the shaft, which he attributes to the death of the man who would have removed him and not to luck. He has entered both explanations and kept the slate.

When a lowlander boy arrives in Keld with a branching red flower across his ribs and an ear that hears a faulty vessel through a wall, Siv will not know what the mark means. He will know that the same shape was crossed out in Y.H. 588 and written in the margin of a Low Kiln slate in Y.H. 803, and that the world has an unlucky habit of producing the same man in different vessels. He will tell the boy the numbers, which is all he has, and the boy will hear something in them that Siv has never heard, because the boy knows what a spiral is.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
The scar, and the twenty years of numbers that do not resolve into anything a Court will print. He fears the Sweating, which he has smelled twice and dreamt of a hundred times, and fears more than that the day the Vorr finally translate the oldest repairs and the translation turns out to be a thing the Keeping has already entered under another name. He fears that he has spent his life measuring a wound and will die without having said what it is, which is the fate of a surveyor and, in Keld, an honourable one. His contradiction: the man who enters everything has kept one thing — the shape of the mark — outside all his tallies, on the back, where only another surveyor would think to look.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the numbers to be right and the shaft to keep its record after he is gone, which in Keld is a form of immortality available to a man with no house. He thinks he wants the translation. What he needs is to understand that a repair made of unkept promises is still a repair, and that the world has been held together by the very thing the Court’s economy produces, which is a thought a surveyor can enter but cannot act on without becoming something other than a surveyor. He will one day give the boy the crossed-out page, and it will be the first thing in his life he has given away rather than entered.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Hald Iron} — master of the floor, who respects the man’s patience and distrusts what he finds. They have shared the shape of the mark in silence, each copying it onto the back of a tally, and neither has said what they think, which is how Keldish men show that a thing is too large for speech.

\textbf{Corin} — the lowlander with the flower. Siv will not tell him what the mark means because he does not know, and will show him the crossed-out page because the boy is the first person who might. The tension is a gift given by a man who has spent his life keeping, to a boy who will read it with an ear the surveyor does not have.

\textbf{The Vorr} — Ao of the Third Seam and Iyn of the Listening Stone, who sit against the stones and come away with numbers. Siv is patient with them in a way no other Keldish man is, because he has learned that a one-sentence answer can be a whole answer if you write it down exactly.

\textbf{Karric VII, Warden of the Rim} — the ruler who keeps the true-Hearth claim for reasons of trade. Siv serves the claim without believing it, which in Keld is not hypocrisy but a way of keeping the shaft open.

\textbf{Rusk of the Weir} — a lowlander who asked him once, twenty years ago, whether the oldest repairs were made of language. Siv said the numbers were correct and told him nothing else, and has wondered ever since whether the man asked the same question of the stones.

\textbf{Marra} — whose name appears in the margin of a Low Kiln slate in Y.H. 803 beside a shape Siv recognises. He has never shown that slate to anyone and has copied the shape into his tally, which is the closest thing to a friendship the Hearthseat will ever have with the First Seam.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters in the survey shaft, listening to a wall before he looks at the visitor, and gives a lowlander boy a number where the boy expected an answer.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 200 (the First Seam, the crossed-out page).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Rennick}{the assessor who can add}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Assessor Rennick, Rennick, the young man with the good hand}\CRfield{Role}{rival}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 786, the Hearthseat, on the Keel}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; officer of the Keeping)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Hearthseat; slate-name entered on the rolls, no house and no kin}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the College of Assessors; posted to Slate Row, Sill, in Y.H. 810}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 300}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Twenty-six, narrow, with the pressed, maintained look of a man who irons his cuffs before he goes to a district where there is no ironing. His hands are good hands — quick, clean, certain on a slate — and he has the student’s habit of tapping the chalk once against the frame before he writes, which the hearth-maids of Low Kiln have learned to read as a signal that a number is about to be entered against somebody. He wears the College’s black wool even in Silt, when a sensible man would wear clay-stained linen, and it marks him as a visitor from the Hearthseat in a way he is unaware of or has decided not to mind. His face is open and slightly eager, which is the least threatening expression in Sarn and, on him, the most dangerous, because it disarms people a moment before the figure lands. He smells of new chalk, clean paper and, when he has been in the Weighing Hall, the Hearth-fire, which he has never mentioned to anyone in Sill.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Fast, precise, quietly delighted by his own accuracy. He says \emph{the figure} where another man would say \emph{the truth}, and he has a way of offering a correct number as if it were a gift, which it is, to the person who asked for it and not to the person it is about. He does not sneer. He does not need to; his certainty does the work. When he is beaten — and he will be beaten — he becomes curious rather than angry, which is worse for the victor, because curiosity is a hand that keeps hold. He has one verbal tic: he repeats the last figure of another person’s statement back to them, quietly, as a way of checking both the figure and the person.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “One in twelve. I’ve checked the notice, the roll and the factor’s copy. They agree. Three copies agreeing is a kind of truth in my trade.”

\item “Forty. I keep coming back to forty. Why forty unspent measures for one intact one? I’ve asked the provost. He said the account stands.”

\item “You heard the flat note before I tapped it. That’s a good trick. I’d like to know how you did it. Not to take it. I want to know whether it’s a method.”

\item “I’m not your enemy. That’s the thing nobody understands about me. I’m a man with a slate. The slate is the enemy, if there has to be one, and even the slate is only following the figure.”

\item “Seven. Six. Five — you’ve lost count, haven’t you? I counted for both of us. That’s my gift, I suppose.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born in the Hearthseat and entered on the rolls at nineteen, which in the College means he has never had a house-name to be proud of or ashamed of. He proved early that he could add, which in the College of Assessors is the rarest of gifts: most officers memorise the figures, a few compute them, and almost none check the printed ones against the rolls. Rennick checked, at twenty-one, and found that one in twelve on the notice did not agree with the deduction on a Fen household’s slate, and that the difference was not an error but a method. He asked Provost Wessen about it. Wessen said the account stands and gave him a figure for his trouble, which Rennick has entered in his private tally as the first kindness shown to him by the institution.

He was posted to Slate Row in Sill in Y.H. 810, at twenty-four, to value the lives of a district whose households he cannot pronunciation-proof and whose children he cannot keep track of. He has kept his own count of the Forty of Low Kiln, which is forty-one, because on the night of the levy he saw a covered bundle carried up the Clay Yard that did not appear on any list, and a collector’s slate with a name written before a face. He cannot spell the name but he remembers the shape of it, and he has begun to look for that shape in every document that passes across his table. He has not told anyone about the forty-first. He has told Wessen about the forty, who told him to enter it as an irregularity and forget it, which he entered and did not forget.

He is good at his work and he likes being good at it, which is his great disqualification. He will meet the foundling Corin as a boy with an ear and no ledger, and will recognise in him a rival in the only sense Rennick understands: a person whose method produces correct answers by a route the College cannot teach. He will try to learn the route, honestly, by asking, and will be refused, and will keep asking, because a refusal is a thing with a shape and he wants to know what it is.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He can add, and nobody in his institution wants him to. His wound is a gift that his teachers have spent six years trying to correct out of him, and his fear is that he will succeed at last and become an ordinary officer who prints one in twelve and never asks why. He fears a person who can hear a vessel without a tool, because such a person makes his arithmetic look like a slow, clumsy way of doing the same thing. He fears the grey fingers of Collector Marra, whose reports are more exact than his and whose exactness he has begun to suspect is a kind of concealment. His contradiction: the young man who believes figures are a form of honesty has learned to hide the one figure he cannot explain, and calls the hiding \emph{an irregularity entered in the proper form}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants to be right in a room where being right is noticed. He thinks he wants the foundling’s method, because a method that hears a cracked vessel through a wall would make the College’s work faster and truer, and perhaps make the true deduction public without anyone having to say who took it. What he needs is to learn that a figure is not a person and that adding a district correctly is not the same as being kind to it, which is the lesson the Warm Court has built eight hundred years on not learning. He will learn it late, and will become more dangerous when he does, because he will then be an assessor who can add and knows who pays.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the rival. Rennick admires the boy’s ear without understanding it and has begun to suspect that the boy hears things he is not supposed to hear, which makes him want to check him the way one checks a slate. The tension is admiration curdling into a need to verify: Rennick will not be able to leave the boy’s method alone, and the boy will not be able to explain it without lying.

\textbf{Wessen} — his teacher, who corrected the verb out of him and refused to correct the arithmetic, which Rennick has decided means the provost wants him to keep adding. He is right about this and wrong about why.

\textbf{Marra} — the collector whose reports are the standard he measures himself against and whose fingers are going grey. He has begun to watch her hands when she writes, which she has noticed and pretended not to.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — who tells him one in twelve in a moot and shakes hands afterwards. Rennick has checked the Thane’s figures three times and found them correct in the way a printed notice is correct, which is to say: agreeable and wrong.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — who answers his questions about births with the weather. He has begun to suspect the weather is an answer and has entered the suspicion nowhere, because an assessor who writes down what a hearth-maid means is an assessor who has started to add in the wrong direction.

\textbf{Steward Derren} — whom he has never met and whose signature he recognises on the private-authorisation of a whole return with no purchaser named. Forty. One. He keeps coming back to it.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters at a table in Slate Row, tapping chalk against the frame, offering a correct figure as a gift to a household that will pay for it.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 300 (Slate Row, the account-day after the boy’s first refusal).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Berr Clay}{the man who signs the levy in a neat hand}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Berr, Berr Clay of the Cross, the Thane of Clay, the Thane}\CRfield{Role}{antagonist}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 764, High Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic)}\CRfield{Origin}{High Kiln, Sill, Grath; a kiln-master’s house}\CRfield{Allegiance}{Kiln-Masters of Sill; Thane of Clay, confirmed by the Court’s factor at Greybank}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 3}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Forty-eight, heavy in the way a wagoner is heavy, with a back that has carried clay and a grey stubble he trims with a knife before market-day. He has a ring of lead-glaze green at the corner of his mouth from the old kiln-master’s habit of tasting glaze, and he cannot stop doing it in front of people, which the masters read as thoroughness and Sella reads as nerves. He wears a cord of red wool across his chest when he sits as Thane and takes it off before he goes home, folding it into a drawer. His hands are large and clean; he wipes his thumb on his apron before he touches a slate so that the slate will stay clean. He smells of hot clay, lead and, on Court business, nothing at all, which is the most expensive smell in Grath.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
The kiln-master’s proverb braided with the Court’s passive grammar. He never says \emph{I} when he can say \emph{the district} or \emph{the account}, and he never raises his voice, which in a moot is more frightening than shouting because the crowd has to lean in to hear him. He apologises in the form of an instruction. He uses the phrase \emph{as the notice says} the way another man might cross himself.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The levy is called. It is not called by me.”

\item “One in twelve, as the notice says. If your household has heard otherwise, your household has heard wrong.”

\item “You can bring the slate to the moot. The moot will shout. The slate will not change.”

\item “Sella. Your widow’s mark is confirmed by the factor at Greybank. That is a kindness, and kindness is entered.”

\item “I signed for the Hearth. I did not sign for a name. There’s a difference, and you can take it to the Cross if you like.”

\item “The figure stands as read. There’ll be a slate at the watch if the house wants it again.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born in High Kiln, the son of a glaze-mixer, and worked up through the yards until the masters chose him Thane of Clay and the Court’s factor at Greybank confirmed it, which is the only way a Thane is confirmed. He has kept the Sill kilns firing through two bad winters and a war of deductions that he describes, when pressed, as \emph{a disagreement about arithmetic}. In Y.H. 811, when the Long Cold had the district calling itself walking thin, he raised the levy to one in three on the factor’s instruction and announced it as one in twelve, because the printed notices say one in twelve and the notices are what a man can be seen reading. He has read the difference in a locked box in his own house. He has never destroyed the box.

In the first weeks of Stone, Y.H. 812, he signed the slate that took forty children of Low Kiln for a private account. The factor at Greybank showed him the purchaser’s name and Berr has forgotten it on purpose, which is a thing a man can do if he is sufficiently determined and sufficiently afraid. He told the district the warmth was for the Hearth, and to a mother named Dessa Silt he said one in twelve and meant one in three. His daughter came back thin in Y.H. 800 and does not remember him; he has kept her name on a slate at home and has never corrected the deduction on her return, because the return is what the household was given and a household does not look a gift in the mouth.
