\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
A daughter who calls him by another man’s name when she is feverish. He fears being seen clearly by Sella, who knew his wife and knows the difference between a levy and a theft. He fears that the locked box will outlive him and that his son will open it. He fears, most of all, that the district will one day ask him why forty children were necessary when one intact measure should have served, because he has an answer ready and knows it will not balance. His contradiction: the man who counts warmth as a public good has privately bought a whole return for his granddaughter and paid for it with the deduction from Low Kiln, and calls the purchase \emph{a gift from the Hearth}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the kilns firing, his name on the roll, and the factor at Greybank to write back in the same hand next year. He thinks he wants the Court’s confirmation. What he needs is to learn that he is the most frightening thing in Sarn — not a dark lord, but a reasonable man with a ledger and a good reason — and that the reason is the part that will be remembered against him.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Sella Vail} — he confirmed her widow’s mark and has never called in the debt Garl left against a shipment of tiles. The uncalled debt is worse than a called one: she cannot pay it, cannot settle it, and cannot stop owing it. She has never asked, and he has decided this means she knows. \emph{Ch 3:} he told her at the Cross that the mark was confirmed by the factor at Greybank, that it was a kindness, and that kindness is entered. She answered the wool and the boy, not him; he stood with his mouth open on the next part of what he had come to say, looked at the mark under her collar without asking to see it, and went back to the moot leaving the debt as carefully unentered as he had found it.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — he suspects the young man is with the Broken Slates and lets him be. A district needs somewhere to put its anger, and Berr has calculated that a kiln-house with a safe place for anger is cheaper than a kiln-house without one. Jorra threw a stone at his door in Y.H. 810; Berr had the stone swept up and does not mention it.

\textbf{Garl Vail} — dead friend and rival. Berr let the Vail house carry a debt rather than call it in, and has spent a year being unable to decide whether this was mercy or the simplest way to keep a witness quiet. \emph{Ch 3:} the debt against the firing of tiles went spoken around at the Cross, a space in a sentence shaped like the hollow seat up the yard; his care not to call it was another kindness entered nowhere.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — he pays her to keep the birth-slates and has never asked about the second notes. He has concluded that she must be paid enough not to keep them, which is an expensive conclusion and a wrong one. \emph{Ch 3:} she kept the birth-slates in a row at the edge of the market, turning the writing down when a mother came near; he did not see the crossed sign she made at the boy’s wrist or the date she covered it with, which is the whole point of paying someone to keep a record.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — he took one in three from her wages and told her one in twelve. She spat at his door and denied it; he had the spittle cleaned before the market opened and never said who did it.

\textbf{Corin} — he watches the boy with a kiln-master’s eye and has decided he is worth more than he costs, which is exactly how the Court will one day see him. This is not cruelty in Berr’s account. It is an investment. \emph{Ch 3:} the boy lay in a basket at the edge of his square all morning and he never once looked into it. The figure was read over the top of him twice; the district went back to the pigs. He has entered this nowhere, including in his own mind.

\textbf{Ch 5.} At the Fishstairs on Stone 14 he came down the steps with the red wool cord folded under his coat and wiped his thumb on his apron before touching his slate, the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth. He said \emph{the levy is called for the feast; it is not called by me}, accepted four eels where six should go without appearing to count them, and gave out \emph{three days to the Five} from the top of the steps with a finger passed over the figure. He has paid her two marks short for six years and taken two eels short for six years, and the war has never been named by either of them. The box in his house stays locked; the true deduction stays in it.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
He enters at the Account-day moot of Stone 10, Y.H. 812, wiping his thumb on his apron before touching the slate, and says \emph{one in twelve, as the notice says} in a low voice that makes the shouting stop because the crowd has to lean in. He never says \emph{I}; he says the district, the account and the notice, and apologises in the form of an instruction. He sets the slate down twice and twice it answers flat, and he does not hear what the boy hears. Later, at the Slate Row end of the Cross, he confirms Sella’s widow’s mark as a kindness that is entered, leaves Garl’s debt against the tiles uncalled, and goes back to the moot with the red cord folded square. The locked box in his house has not been shown or named. He has been on the page for one chapter and is already the local face of the Court: reasonable, clean-handed, with a good reason for every cruelty and no idea that he has given any.

On Stone 14 the Thane comes down to the eel trade and is the same man in a market that he was at the Cross: the clean thumb, the passive grammar that takes the speaker out of his own sentence, the hand that counts a figure before he lets it go. He calls the levy for the feast and says it is not called by him, and takes four eels where six should go and does not seem to notice, which is how a man can be short and kind and reasonable in the same breath. The number of days to the Five is let out into the market air and is still nothing anybody can spend.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 3: Day 4, Account-day. At the Cross, before two hundred people and eight pigs, wiped his thumb on his apron before touching the clean slate and read \emph{one in twelve, as the notice says} in the voice that stops the roar; added that a household which has heard otherwise has heard wrong, that the account stands as read, and that there will be a slate at the watch for any household that wants the figure again. Read it a second time in the afternoon for the households that came late. Set the slate down twice; both times it gave its small flat answer, unremarked. Wore the red wool cord of his office folded square and tasted his own glaze without appearing to. At the Slate Row end confirmed Sella’s widow’s mark as a kindness entered by the factor at Greybank, spoke around the uncalled debt against the firing of tiles, did not ask to see the mark, and left the debt as he found it. Did not look into the basket and never addressed the boy.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Day 8, at the Fishstairs. Came down the steps with the red wool cord of his office folded under his coat, hands large and clean, thumb wiped on his apron before he touched his slate, lead-glaze green at one corner of his mouth and hot clay over the smell of nothing. Said \emph{the levy is called for the feast; it is not called by me} to the eels, accepted four eels where six should go, and gave out \emph{three days to the Five} from the top of the steps with a finger passed over the figure. Did not acknowledge the two eels short against his two marks short of six years and did not look into the basket.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Marra}{the woman at the edge of the flare light}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Collector Marra, Marra of the Grey Coat, the woman with the slate, the hand that writes}\CRfield{Role}{antagonist}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 768, the Hearthseat, on the Keel}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; officer of the Keeping)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Hearthseat; no house, no kin; entered on the rolls in Ledgerhand}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Warm Court, collector’s office; secretly the Unbanked}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 1}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
In the death scene she is a grey shape at the edge of the flare light in a coat the colour of wet ash, holding a slate, writing; the writing does not stop when the van takes him. In Sarn she is a woman in her forties with the neat, unremarkable face of an officer who has spent her life at tables rather than in rooms. Her fingertips are the colour of wet ash and have been since Y.H. 803, which she attributes to cold rooms in a voice that invites no question. She wears her hair pinned tight and her cuffs buttoned to the wrist, partly from the trade and partly because she does not want the hand seen. She has the collector’s habit of touching the slate before she touches the person, and a smaller habit — noticed only by people who have watched her for years — of touching her own wrist afterwards, where the iron clip would go, as if checking that it is not there. She smells of cold iron, slate dust and nothing else, which is the most expensive smell in Sarn.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
She apologises in clauses she does not finish. She is precise about other people’s warmth and vague about her own, and the vagueness is not evasion but a genuine absence: she has entered so many measures that she has lost the habit of entering herself. She speaks the Ledgerhand passive as naturally as breathing — \emph{the measure is banked, the return is recorded, the account stands} — and then, rarely, a plain sentence breaks through, usually about a child, and is immediately corrected into the grammar again. When she lies she becomes even more exact, giving dates and figures that nobody asked for.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The measure was drawn at the fourth beat. I’m sorry, I — the account stands. That’s all that’s required.”

\item “I don’t remember my fingers. I mean, I remember that they were — they were warm once. That isn’t in the report.”

\item “You were written before you were seen. That’s an irregularity. It isn’t a cruelty. There’s a difference, and I’ve entered it.”

\item “Forty measures. One return. If you add it and it doesn’t balance, you’re adding it wrong. I’ve added it. I know what it balances to.”

\item “I’ve been paying since. Don’t ask me with what. It isn’t in the report.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
She entered the Keeping as a collector’s assistant in Greybank in Y.H. 790, arriving from the Hearthseat with a slate and an unusual care for the measurement of other people’s warmth. Her early reports were models of the craft: exact, unembellished, quiet about her own deductions. Within four years she was collecting in the Sill district; within twenty her fingertips had begun to go grey; within twenty-five she was the collector of the Low Kiln levy and the woman the district crosses the street to avoid. She has never been reprimanded and has never been promoted, which is a distinction she has thought about more carefully than either.

In the winter of Y.H. 812 she was standing at the edge of a flare light on an expressway in a country she could not have named, writing because the trace in front of her would not cool. She did not cause the crash and did not touch the child. She wrote the measure of a man who gave at the moment of breaking and whose promise was too heavy to bank. She wrote a name — Corin — before she had seen the infant’s face. Then she went home to Sarn and forged the assessment: a whole return with memory intact, booked at twelve hundred marks and forty unspent measures, with the deduction entered as zero. The money came from forty children of Low Kiln. The collector’s clerk who carried the slate has never been able to spell the buyer’s name.

She has been paying for the return ever since with her own fingers, a little warmth at a time, drawn without a slate and booked nowhere. In Y.H. 803 a slate in Greybank was found with a line that did not balance; it was corrected in a second hand and filed without comment. The first hand was hers. The second was also hers, written with the other hand so that the file would show two officers.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
Her fingers. And underneath them, the forty. She can add and she has added, and the difference between what a whole return costs and what the Hearth was told it cost is a number she carries the way Dan carries Tasha’s chart — as a small, hard, private knowledge of the shape of her own conduct. She fears the Weighing, where the year’s measures are read aloud in a clean voice, because one of them is hers and it is not on any roll. She fears that the boy will one day look at her with that clinical attention and add her up the way she has added up everyone else. Her contradiction: the woman who invented an officer’s exactness to hide a forgery has made the forgery the most exact thing in her life, and calls it \emph{the account standing}.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
She wants the sealed world too — the Unbanked’s paradise, where nothing leaks and nothing is owed — and she wants the instrument they sent for to be whole enough to do the sealing. She thinks she wants the boy to live. What she needs is to understand that she has already spent him: forty children bought one man, and no amount of careful handwriting makes that a balance. When she finally confesses it, it will be in a clause she does not finish, and the boy will finish it for her, in the plainest words, and the world will not end.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — the return she paid for and has never watched grow. She knows his name before she knows his face, and when she meets him in Slate Row she will look at him the way a surgeon looks at a chart: to check whether her own work held. He will be the first person to ask her about the fingers, and the first to hear her say \emph{I don’t remember} and understand that she means \emph{I do}. \emph{Ch 1:} she is a coat the colour of wet ash at the edge of a flare, a hand moving on a slate, and the same pause in a dark hand on a birth-slate in Low Kiln. He has filed the hand without a name and has not put the two together.

\textbf{Rusk} — the other half of the purchase. He taught the boy; she bought him. They have not spoken plainly in twelve years and have arranged their lives so that they never have to. She has begun to be afraid that he is gentler with the boy than the work permits, which in her vocabulary is the same as saying he has begun to love him.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — she took his signature on the levy of forty and let him forget the purchaser’s name, which he did deliberately and with relief. She has never thanked him and never will; a debt acknowledged is a debt entered.

\textbf{Alda Reed} — Alda crossed herself at a birth-flower and wrote nothing in the Court’s book. Marra has read the Court’s book. She has never asked Alda what else she wrote, because a hearth-maid’s second note is the one thing even a collector cannot lawfully want.

\textbf{Haldre} — whose paper named the ending she has been paying for. She has never read it. She has heard it described once, by Rusk, and has spent the years since doing the work without the theory, which is a more Sarnish arrangement than either of them will admit.

\textbf{Steward Derren} — her head of office, whom she has never contradicted and never trusted. He has praised her exactness twice. She has decided that his praise is the most dangerous thing in the Hearthseat.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
Chapter 1 gives the first of her two entrances before she enters. She is a grey shape at the edge of the flare light: a coat, a head, a hand, writing without stopping while the van burns. She is not named, not to Dan and not to the reader, who is allowed to think she caused the fire. The writing has the same rhythm of short strokes and a decided pause as the strange dark hand on the birth-slate in House Silt, and Dan sets the two side by side without putting them together. The fingers, the forged assessment, the forty and the unfinished apology are all still buried.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Appears only as the image at the edge of the flare light --- coat the colour of wet ash, hand on a slate, short strokes, a pause, more strokes, the writing not stopping when the fire takes the van. Unnamed in the Sarn narration and unremembered by name. She wrote a name before anyone saw the child’s face; by morning that pause is sitting on a slab of grey stone within reach of a midwife’s hand.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Ulda}{the friend who knew exactly what to say}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Ulda, Ulda of the Fishstairs, the foundling with the ready laugh, the boy who sold the mark}\CRfield{Role}{antagonist}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 809, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath (foundling; one name only)}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; foundling, one name only)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; no house, no kin, no slate until the Court entered him}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Fishstairs; the Waiting Room clerk; quietly, the keeping of small secrets for people who pay}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 6}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Three years older than Corin and twice as easy in the world, with a narrow face that is always slightly amused and a set of clothes that are somebody else’s cast-offs worn as if they were a uniform. He has a gap where a front tooth was knocked out at the Fishstairs and he grins through it, which makes people forgive the things he says before they have decided whether to be offended. He keeps a sliver of green lead-glaze in a string around his neck, picked up in High Kiln, and rubs it with his thumb while he decides what a person wants to hear, which is almost always the thing they have already said they want and a little more. He smells of river silt, small ale and the warm stone of other people’s hearths, because he is always in somebody’s kitchen and never in his own. The thing people notice first is the grin; the thing they notice last is that when Corin turns his head toward a pot, Ulda turns it too, a half-beat late, and has never once been able to explain why.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Fast, warm, fluent in the proverb-form of Low Kiln and quicker than the adults at it. He is the only child in the yard who can make the kiln-hands laugh, and he does it by saying the thing the adults are thinking in a voice that makes it harmless. He never says \emph{I want}; he says \emph{they say}, \emph{everyone knows}, \emph{your house wouldn’t mind}, which is a way of wanting without owning the want. When he is lying he gets affectionate, which is the tell that Mira Silt has learned to read and that Corin, for years, took for kindness. He has a habit of finishing another person’s sentence for them, correctly, and then looking surprised that it was not what they meant.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Everyone knows the Vail boy isn’t Vail. I’m not saying it. I’m saying everyone knows it. There’s a difference.”

\item “You heard it before I tapped it, didn’t you? That’s not a trick. That’s a thing you should be careful with, Corin. I’m only saying.”

\item “I didn’t tell the clerk. I told him a thing and he wrote it down. That’s not the same, is it? You tell me, because I can never tell which part’s mine.”

\item “The mark. Under your sleeve. I saw it at the Weir and I didn’t say. I didn’t say for three years, Corin. That’s what I’m asking you to remember.”

\item “Sella’s kind to you. She’s kind to me too, when she’s thinking of something else. Don’t make it more than it is.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born in Low Kiln to a mother entered in the name-pot and returned thin, and he has never known which of the several dead in him is his mother, which he describes as \emph{a family, in a manner of speaking}. He was entered on a slate at three because the Court began counting foundlings in Y.H. 782, and has had a name on a roll ever since, which makes him, in his own account, a man of property compared with Corin, who for his first years had none. He attached himself to the Vail yard at five because there was alwaysbread there and because the boy with the flower was interesting in a way Ulda could not name. He became the foundling’s friend in the way poor children become friends: by sharing a crust, by knowing which boards at the Fishstairs are safe, and by being the person who says the thing aloud that the other one is thinking.

He has sold small secrets at the Fishstairs since he was nine — which household has paid the levy, which collector takes his ale before a draw, which hearth-maid has begun to keep a second set of notes — and has never thought of it as betrayal, because a secret in Low Kiln is a thing that belongs to whoever can use it. In Y.H. 811 he told a Waiting Room clerk that a foundling under the Vail roof could hear the full note of a pot before it was tapped. The clerk wrote it down. Ulda was eleven and was paid in a loaf, and has entered the loaf in no account, which is the only thing about the whole business he has never been able to explain to himself.

Three years later he will tell the same clerk about the branching red mark under the boy’s sleeve, which he saw at the Weir and has kept for exactly the right moment, and the clerk will write that down too. He will do it because he has begun to be afraid that Corin will one day be taken by the Court and Ulda will not be there to be useful, which in his vocabulary is the same as being abandoned. He will say afterwards that he did it to protect him. He will believe it for a while.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He has no house and has made a career of being the person who fits into other people’s. His wound is that he cannot want anything in his own voice: every desire comes out as \emph{they say} or \emph{everyone knows}, so that he can deny it if it is refused. He fears the name-pot, because a foundling entered on a slate can be entered again, and fears more than that the day Corin looks at him with that clinical, quiet attention and sees the thing he did at eleven. His contradiction: the boy who tells everyone’s secrets has kept one of his own for three years — the mark — and calls the keeping of it loyalty, when it is a thing he has been saving to spend.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants a place in a household, any household, where the door closes on him and somebody says his name without a slate in front of them. He thinks he wants Corin to stay in Low Kiln and be his friend forever, which is the same as wanting the boy not to become what he is becoming. What he needs is to understand that a person cannot be kept by being useful, and that the loaf he was paid at eleven has gathered interest for three years in an account he cannot read. When he finally says the thing he did, it will be in the affectionate voice, and it will be the first honest sentence he has ever spoken, and Corin will not believe him at first, which is the correct response.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Corin} — his friend, the only person he has never tried to manage, which is why the betrayal will work. Ulda knows the boy hears things and has spent years pretending not to notice so that the boy will keep being ordinary beside him. The tension is a love that expresses itself as a careful inventory of the beloved’s weaknesses, held in reserve.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — she reads his affection as the tell it is and has told him once, at the Fishstairs, that a boy who sells a secret has no way of buying it back. He answered with the weather and has never forgotten that she did not laugh.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — he carries messages for her and has never once told her that the boy she thinks is dead is three doors away, because a secret that big would make him important, and a boy who is important gets entered on a slate.

\textbf{Sella Vail} — he is always in her kitchen and she has never once said he may not be, which he has decided is the same as being wanted and is not.

\textbf{The Waiting Room clerk} — a man whose name is not on any roll, who pays in loaves and ale and has taught Ulda that a secret is worth exactly what somebody will give for it. This is the man who will buy the mark.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — who would throw him out of the yard if he knew half of it, and whom Ulda watches carefully, because Jorra has broken three birth-slates and is therefore a person who could be useful or a person who could be dangerous.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters as the grin in the clay yard, finishing a kiln-hand’s sentence for him, turning his head toward a pot half a beat after the foundling does.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 6 (the Clay Yard, the shared crust).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Steward Derren}{the reasonable man at the head of the account}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Steward Derren, Derren, the head of the Keeping, the voice that never rises}\CRfield{Role}{villain}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 749, the Hearthseat, on the Keel}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; officer of the Keeping)}\CRfield{Origin}{the Hearthseat; slate-name entered on the rolls, no house and no kin}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Warm Court — Steward of the Hearth; keeper of the sealed files}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 640}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Sixty-three and spare, with the dry, maintained look of a vessel that has been carefully tempered and has never been allowed to run cold. He dresses in the Hearthseat’s plain dark wool with a strip of lead-grey at the collar that denotes the office and nothing else; he has no rings, no house-mark and no ornament, because an officer who wears a thing gives the rolls something to read. His hands are clean to the point of aggression — the hands of a man who has not carried fuel in forty years and does not need to. His voice is low, level and slightly warm, which is the most frightening thing about him, because his listeners must lean in and therefore agree to the sentence before it is finished. He has a trick of resting his fingertips on a slate while he speaks, not to consult it but to keep it flat, the way another man might keep a cup from sliding. He smells of warm stone, clean paper and the faintest trace of the Hearth-fire, which follows him from the Weighing Hall and which he has never tried to hide.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Ledgerhand grammar made flesh: every sentence passive, every verb removed, every cruelty in the third person. He never raises his voice, never says \emph{I} where he can say \emph{the Keeping}, and never says \emph{you} where he can say \emph{the account}. He has eleven words for a deduction and will use the exact one, which is his way of being honest within the only vocabulary he considers real. When he is contradicted he becomes interested, which is worse than anger. He has one habit that his officers have learned to fear: he writes the thing down while the person is still speaking.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The measure is banked. The account stands. Nothing is lost that we have kept. That is the whole of it, and it is not a threat.”

\item “You have said a thing that cannot be entered. I have entered it anyway. That is my kindness.”

\item “The world cannot be saved. The counting can be continued. Those are different propositions, and only one of them is my work.”

\item “One in twelve is printed on the notice. What a household believes is not in the notice, and is therefore not in the Keeping.”

\item “I have read the sealed file. You have not. Shall I tell you what it says, or shall we go on as we were?”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was entered on the rolls at twenty-two and has never held another name. He rose not by cleverness but by an unbroken capacity to be present when a difficult slate had to be signed: the Ash Rising of Y.H. 668, the suppression of Haldre’s paper in 710, the sealing of the First Seam survey in 806, the Long Cold of 811. In each case he was the officer who wrote the final line in a hand that did not vary, and in each case the line was a kindness in the grammar of the Keeping — the district was quieted, the paper was suppressed, the survey was sealed, the levy was raised for the Hearth. He has never been accused of cruelty and has never understood an accusation when one was made, because he cannot see the person inside the account, and has spent forty years perfecting the not-seeing.

He has read the sealed file. He knows the Hearth is a wound and the Keeping is salvage from a failing repair. He knows the deduction in Low Kiln is one in three and that the printed notice is a founding invoice dressed as mercy. He knows the oldest repairs under the world are neither clay nor stone, and he knows what the Keldish translation and the Court translation of them were: both guesses, both near. He has read Haldre’s paper and has not burned his copy. He keeps it because it is the only honest document in the Hearthseat and because he has decided that honesty, left where it is, does no harm. He is not a believer. He is a continuance.

In the first weeks of Stone, Y.H. 812, he authorised a private whole return of forty unspent measures without recording the purchaser. He did this personally and with perfect composure, and has entered nothing about it anywhere except in the sealed file, where the entry reads: \emph{The instrument is in place. The account is carried.}

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He has no wound he will admit to, which is itself the wound: a man who has trained himself not to feel the cold has become unable to feel anyone else’s. He fears the Waste growing inward, because it is the only thing in his ledger that does not balance and cannot be written down as a kindness. He fears a hot trace that will not cool, because it is the only measure that will not enter an account. He fears, most of all, a man who looks at a dying body and says plainly \emph{leave it}, because that sentence has no passive form and therefore cannot be entered, and a thing that cannot be entered is a thing the Keeping cannot survive. His contradiction is not hidden and not a secret: he knows the world cannot be saved by the Keeping, and he continues the Keeping anyway, and calls the continuation mercy.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants the account to continue. He thinks he wants the world kept. What he needs is to understand the difference — which he already knows and has chosen to forget — and that a man who continues a failing repair is not keeping the world but spending it, one district at a time. He will never understand this in any way that changes him. He will understand it perfectly in the way that lets him write it down.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Marra} — his collector, the most exact hand in his service, whose fingers are going grey and whose reports have never once mentioned her own deductions. He has praised her twice and once asked her, gently, whether she believed the printed figure. She said the account stood. He has not asked again, because the answer confirmed what he needed: she is one of his own, in the sense that she has learned not to see the person.

\textbf{Derren the Younger} — an officer’s son with access to the sealed file and a different last name in his heart. The younger man has begun to ask about the oldest repairs in language that is almost the Deep Hand. Steward Derren has not stopped him, which is the most dangerous permission in the Hearthseat.

\textbf{Pell Varr} — the Assessor-Marshal who put down the Ash Rising by list. They are the two men who understand what a district costs, and they have never once discussed it, because a discussion would require the word \emph{I}.

\textbf{Haldre} — the keeper who vanished in Y.H. 704. Steward Derren keeps Haldre’s paper in a drawer beside the sealed file and reads it on the Last Day, when the ash in the corners is not swept. He has decided that Haldre was right and that being right changes nothing.

\textbf{Rusk} — a name in a sealed file, a person of the sealed opinion, whom the Steward has never met and has caused to be watched for thirty years without ever ordering an arrest. He likes the man’s questions and has said so in a report, which is the closest thing to affection in the Hearthseat.

\textbf{Corin} — not yet a name in his account, only an irregularity in a slate from Greybank: a whole return whose deduction is zero. He has read that slate twice and has written nothing in the margin, which for him is an act of extraordinary restraint and the beginning of an interest.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters in a room gone cold — a single scene away from Dan, in the Weighing Hall or a counting room — where a slate is read aloud and a man is quietly entered. The reader will feel the shift in the prose before they know why.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 640 (a Court scene away from Dan).

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Tarn Vail}{the debt with hands}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Tarn, the Vail’s Tarn, Tarn of the Yard}\CRfield{Role}{other}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 790, Low Kiln, Sill, Grath}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Grathic; returned thin)}\CRfield{Origin}{Low Kiln, Sill, Grath; no house of his own}\CRfield{Allegiance}{House Vail, clay-bound for seven years from Y.H. 806}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 2}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Twenty-two and bent at the shoulders from six years in the slip pit, which makes him look older than his term. He has grey Ash in the creases of his knuckles that no amount of scrubbing lifts, and a pale ring round his left wrist where an iron clip sat in Y.H. 806, when he was bound. He is a narrow man with a narrow face and the habit of putting the smallest tool on the bench into his pocket when he thinks nobody is looking, because a man who owns nothing should know where the small things are. He sleeps in the Debt Loft above the wheel-shed and keeps his few possessions in a clay pot with no mark on it. He smells of wet wool, tallow and the sour tang of a corner that has not been swept. The thing people notice first is how quickly he moves; the thing they notice last is that he stops moving altogether when he is told he is doing well.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Deferential with a nail in it. He calls everyone \emph{house}, including Sella, and says \emph{I owe} where another man would say \emph{I am}. He never argues; he agrees so thoroughly that the argument has nowhere to go. When he is frightened he becomes helpful, which in a kiln-house means doing three things at once and quietly putting the fourth right. His jokes are not jokes; they are statements of fact made in a tone that invites someone else to laugh so that he does not have to.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “Yes, house. I’ll sweep it. I swept it this morning. I’ll sweep it again.”

\item “Six years. One to go. I’ve counted every one, and I won’t count the next.”

\item “He’s a foundling. I’m a debt. Don’t put us in the same sentence, Sella.”

\item “I took the small knife. I put it back. You can check.”

\item “You can’t return a man to himself. They tried with my mother. She came back with somebody else’s hands.”

\item “I took the small knife. I put it back. You can check.” --- said of his own accord, to nobody, in the tone of a man stating the weather.

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
His mother was banked in Y.H. 792 and came back thin to the district’s name-pot, with fragments of a life she could not name and hands that frightened him because they were not her hands. He was born of that return, which means he carries a little of somebody else’s interest in a life he cannot place, and has been told all his life that this makes him useful. In Y.H. 806, at sixteen, his mother’s account was entered against him and he was bound to House Vail for seven years, a term he did not choose and has counted every day of. He has worked the slip pits, swept the yard, banked the fire and carried the household’s small accounts in his head for six years.

He was sent to fetch a collector for Garl Vail in the Long Cold and turned back at the frozen ford. Sella never asked whether he had gone. Jorra, who was thirteen, never asked either, which Tarn has decided is worse. He was in the house when Corin arrived and watched the boy be given a bed while he slept in the loft, and he has never said the thing he thinks about it, because a clay-bound hand who complains about beds is a clay-bound hand who can be returned to the slate.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He is a debt with a body, and one year of a seven-year term left. He fears that when the term ends there will be no house to take him and he will be entered again for vagrancy, which is how the district keeps people. He fears the Second Want in himself — the shape that comes over him some nights and leaves him reaching for a tool he has never used. He fears Sella’s kindness more than Jorra’s command, because the kindness is the thing he cannot enter in any account and therefore cannot pay back. His contradiction: the man who owns nothing is the one who notices when a tool is missing and the one who keeps the house’s accounts most exactly.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants a slate with his own name on it and no mark against it. He wants to be called by something that is not a house’s possession. He thinks he wants freedom; what he needs is to learn that freedom in Sarn is another category of account, and that a man can be unowned and still be cold, and that the thing to want is not an empty column but a way to keep warm without being spent.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Sella Vail} — she holds his debt and once, in the winter Garl died, gave him a warm stone for the loft which he has kept in his clay pot for two years. He cannot reconcile the stone with the debt, and has stopped trying, which is not the same as forgiving it. \emph{Ch 2:} he calls her \emph{house}, says \emph{I owe} where another man says \emph{I am}, and agreed with everything so thoroughly that there was nowhere for an argument to go. She gave the foundling a bed and him the loft, and he put the one sentence into the room that nobody had asked for.

\textbf{Jorra Vail} — son of the house, who treats him as a brother when the wheel needs him and as a servant when it does not. Tarn has decided to hate him for it and keeps failing, because Jorra once sat up all night with him through a fever and has never mentioned it. \emph{Ch 2:} Jorra noticed a rasp left out of its place and Tarn answered him; the kindness and the command arrived in the same voice and Tarn did not separate them.

\textbf{Corin} — the foundling who arrived and was given a bed in the house. Tarn was the first to notice that the boy turns his head toward a pot before anyone taps it, and has told nobody, because a clay-bound hand who can hear the full note is worth more than his debt and he does not want to be bought. \emph{Ch 2:} he said \emph{He’s a foundling. I’m a debt. Don’t put us in the same sentence, Sella.} His resentment is not envy of the bed; it is a man keeping the one thing he owns, which is the exact account of what he is owed and what he owes.

\textbf{Dessa Silt} — he has carried messages between her and the house, and barley from her to Sella, and has hated being the thin part of that chain. He knows her cough is not the pits and has never said so.

\textbf{Mira Silt} — she has suspected for a year that he is more than he says, and has not asked. He has noticed her noticing, and has answered her with the weather every time.

\textbf{Garl Vail} — he was sent for a collector and turned back. The dead man’s coat still hangs behind the door and Tarn has never once touched it.

\textbf{Ch 4.} He called Sella \emph{house} and put the knife back twice where it could be checked; his broom stopped two strokes at the Vail pot’s note without a turn of the head, and the boy watched and kept the silence as carefully as he did. Six of seven years counted, the seventh not. Jorra came in from the back room with slate dust and said the back room was cold; Tarn said nothing about that either. Dessa’s cough went by him as weather, as it always did. The one thing he owns stays kept.

\textbf{Ch 5.} On Stone 13 he came up the yard with the knife already back on its sill, went through the motion of checking it with his eyes, and saw the boy turn his head toward a pot \emph{before} it was set down. His broom stopped half a stroke at the soft set-down that gave nothing, and he said nothing about either thing, as he has said nothing about the pot’s note through the wall. He counted six years of the seven and held the seventh back; when Sella told him he had done well he stopped altogether for two breaths, then began again. At dusk he set the knife back where it could be checked and told the yard he had done so, unasked. He wants his exact account and grudges the house the loan of a bed; a loan that can hear is a loan that can be called, and he has said neither sentence.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
He enters already sweeping, before anyone wakes, a bent man of twenty-two with grey Ash in the creases of his knuckles and a pale ring round his left wrist where the clip sat in Y.H. 806. Six years of a seven-year term lie behind him and he has counted every one and will not count the next. In Chapter 2 he pockets the small bench knife and then says of his own accord that he took it and put it back and they can check; he stops his broom for two strokes when a pot is set down and gives its flat note, turns his head a degree and says nothing; he banks the coals without looking at the fire. When the foundling is given a bed and he is given the loft he makes one short sentence of it and goes back to sweeping. His deference has a nail in it, and the nail is the one possession the house cannot check.

On Days 5 and 6 he sweeps twice and says nothing. The small knife goes into his pocket and back onto the sill where it can be checked, announced unasked both times; the broom stops for two strokes when a Vail pot gives its flat note through the wall, and he does not turn his head or name the sound. The reader sees the second ear in the house; nobody under the roof does. Six years of seven are behind him and he will not count the seventh, which is the one entry in the house the house cannot check. He keeps his head down over the broom and the one thing he owns out of the house’s reach.

On Days 7, 8 and 9 the ear is no longer only the reader’s. He sees the boy expect a note from a pot that has not yet been set down, and stops his own broom at the set-down that gives nothing, and the two of them go on under one roof without a word between them --- a loan that can hear now knowing he is not the only one, which makes the thing he owns both more valuable and more dangerous. The small knife goes back to its sill and is announced unasked; six of seven years are counted and the seventh is held back, the one entry in the house the house cannot check. He stops for two breaths when Sella says he has done well and then goes on sweeping, because an argument has nowhere to put its foot when a man agrees with everything.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 1: Does not appear; the broom, the knife and the loft are held for Chapter 2.

\item Ch 2: Swept the kiln-house before anyone woke and stopped for two strokes at a pot’s flat note without turning his head or naming the sound. Put the small bench knife in his pocket, then took it out and put it back where it could be checked and said so unasked. Told Sella \emph{Six years. One to go. I’ve counted every one, and I won’t count the next.} Carried up from the Debt Loft with a broom on the third day, banked the coals, and said \emph{He’s a foundling. I’m a debt. Don’t put us in the same sentence, Sella.} when the boy was given a bed. Heard the second flat note of the three days from a pot set down on the bench and did not say what it was.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 4: Days 5 and 6. Swept before light on Stone 11 and again at dusk on Stone 12, with Ash in his knuckles and the pale ring at his left wrist. Took the small bench knife out and put it back where it could be checked, saying so unasked both times. His broom stopped for two strokes at the flat note of a pot through the shed wall and he did not turn his head, name the sound or say that it was a sound at all --- the second ear in the house, witnessed only by the reader. Said \emph{yes, house; I’ll sweep it; I swept it this morning; I’ll sweep it again} to the ground and kept his head down over the broom. Six of seven years counted, the seventh not counted. He said nothing about the note all day, and nobody asked him to.

\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item Ch 5: Days 7--9. On Stone 13 came up the yard with the small knife already back on its sill, checked it with his eyes only, and saw the boy turn his head toward a pot before Jorra set it down; his broom stopped half a stroke at the set-down that gave nothing and he said nothing about either. Counted six years of seven and held the seventh back, and stopped altogether for two breaths when Sella said he had done well, then swept again. At dusk put the knife back where it could be checked and announced it unasked, and kept his head down over the broom while Sella counted loaves and bits. Said nothing all three days about the note that did not come, and nobody asked him to.

\end{itemize}
}

\CRcharacter{Veno Brine}{the factor who can turn a damp document into a fortune}{}{\CRfield{Also called}{Veno Brine, the factor, Veno of Greybank, the man who never signs anything}\CRfield{Role}{other}\CRfield{Born}{Y.H. 766, Vhal, the Sallow coast}\CRfield{Kind}{human (Vhaleni)}\CRfield{Origin}{Vhal, the Sallow coast; a house with no boat-share and an excellent memory for wet paper}\CRfield{Allegiance}{the Greybank counting house; agent of Sere interests in the north; Council of Tides by correspondence}\CRfield{Status}{alive}\CRfield{First seen}{Chapter 130}}{\CRsheetheading{Appearance}
Forty-six and carefully dressed in a lowland cut that he has never quite got right, which he does not care about because the clothes are for the people he deals with and not for himself. He is a narrow Vhaleni man with a client’s smile that he can hold for an entire negotiation and drop the instant the door closes, which the clerks of Greybank have learned to read as the true end of the meeting. His hands are soft, with salt-paper stains at the fingertips, and he never carries a pen, because a factor who is seen writing is a factor whose word can be checked. He has a gold crown mounted in a ring which he wears on his little finger and which he has never earned in Grath, a detail he considers the only beautiful thing about the place. He smells of tar, wet rope, expensive soap and — when a Sere matter is in hand — myrrh, which is the smell of the empire and of a room where a person has been entered. The thing people notice first is the smile; the thing they notice last is that he never touches a slate, not even to move it, which in a counting house is a form of armour.

\CRsheetheading{Voice \& Manner}
Vhaleni sibilance flattened for lowland ears, with long pauses and questions that contain their own answers. He says \emph{the document}, \emph{the weather}, \emph{the understanding}, and never \emph{the contract}, because a contract is a thing that can be read and he has built his life on things that cannot. He speaks of people as positions — \emph{the widow, thane, the collector} — and has a way of offering kindness with a figure attached, so that the recipient must decide which part was the gift, which is the whole art of his trade. When he is genuinely angered he becomes courteous to a degree that makes the room cold, because everyone has seen what precedes it. He has two laughs: one for clients, warm and brief, and one for himself, which is a single breath through the nose and means he has just been found out and does not intend to admit it.

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item “The paper is damp. It’s a bad season for documents. In Vhalen we consider that a virtue. A law that lasts too long stops earning, doesn’t it?”

\item “I have not paid you. I have advanced you. You’ll see the difference when the grain comes.”

\item “Forty measures for one return. Ask me why forty and I’ll tell you the figure is the figure. Ask me again in private and I’ll still tell you the figure is the figure.”

\item “I don’t buy people. I buy the shape of them. A beautiful return must remember enough to be exhibited. There’s a difference, and the Council calls it civility.”

\item “You want the girl. I want the share. We’ll write it down in the rain and see which of us has a document in the morning.”

\end{itemize}

\CRsheetheading{History}
He was born in Vhal to a family with no boat-share and an unusual talent for reading documents that had been left in the rain, which in the salt republic is the most marketable gift a man can have. He worked the counting house, learned that a contract on salt-paper in damp weather is no contract at all, and spent twelve years turning that fact into a fortune and two enemies, one of whom he later bought out with a damp document and a warm smile. He came up the Salt Road to Greybank in Y.H. 796 as a factor for Vhaleni glass and stayed, because the north buys measures and a factor who can buy a debt before it is due is a factor who never needs to buy the person. He has represented Sere interests in the north since Y.H. 803 without ever admitting to the Sun-Emperor’s Council that he works for them, and has sold grain, glass, quiet ash and whole returns to people who believe they are dealing with a Vhaleni and not an empire.

In the first weeks of Stone, Y.H. 812, he carried the slate for a private whole return from the Greybank factor to the collector in Sill, and could not spell the purchaser’s name, which he has never told anyone and has never forgotten. He has spent the years since trying to work out who bought forty children’s warmth for a foundling with a flower, because a man who cannot identify a buyer is a man who cannot charge him again, which in his profession is a form of insult. He has begun to suspect the Unbanked, then the Court, then a third thing that looks like both, and has kept each suspicion in a different document, all of them damp.

He has kept Solene Ember in a room with good light since Y.H. 810, which he describes to visitors as protection and to himself as an investment that has not yet matured. He has twice offered to buy Pia of the Quay’s mother’s boat-share and been refused twice, which he has entered as a debt owed to him by a girl who does not know she owes it.

\CRsheetheading{Wounds \& Fears}
He has no house and no share, which in Vhalen makes him a man whose account is always current and never his own. His wound is that he can turn any document into nothing and therefore cannot make anything that lasts, which is a strange hunger in a man who deals in permanent arrangements. He fears wet paper in the wrong hands, which is the only thing that can undo him, and fears more than that a person who reads a contract as a person rather than a shape, which is what the foundling from Low Kiln does when he looks at a vessel. His contradiction: the man whose whole trade is the convenient disappearance of evidence has kept three damp documents dry in a private drawer for ten years, because a factor who keeps nothing can never be blackmailed and a factor who keeps everything can, and he has chosen to be the second kind in secret and the first kind in public.

\CRsheetheading{Desires}
He wants a boat-share, which would make him a Vhaleni instead of a man who deals with them, and he wants to know who bought the whole return in Y.H. 812, because an unspelled name is the only account in his life he cannot close. He thinks he wants the north to remain a place where a damp document is a fortune. What he needs is to understand that a person with no account is cheaper than a person with one, which is the Council’s doctrine and his own, and that he has spent his life making people cheaper in order to call himself civility. When he finally learns the purchaser’s name, he will not say it aloud; he will write it on salt-paper in the rain and watch it disappear, which is the only honest thing he has ever done with a document.

\CRsheetheading{Bonds}
\textbf{Solene Ember} — the beautiful return he keeps in a room with good light. He is kind to her in the exact way a creditor is kind, and she has never mistaken it, which is why he keeps her and why he distrusts her. He has begun to fear that she will refuse a fourth return, which would be a loss and a precedent, in that order.

\textbf{Pia of the Quay} — the salt girl who refused his offer for her mother’s share twice. He has entered the refusal as a debt and has never collected, which in his vocabulary is the beginning of an interest he cannot name.

\textbf{Marra} — the collector whose slate passed through his hands in Y.H. 812 and whose name appeared on a document he was not supposed to read. He has not told anyone and has used the knowledge once, gently, in a negotiation, which is how he tests what a thing is worth.

\textbf{Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay} — who signed the levy he carried and forgot the purchaser’s name deliberately. Veno has never pressed him, because a Thane who has forgotten a thing on purpose is a Thane who can be made to remember it at the right moment, which is a kind of leverage he has kept warm for ten years like a coal under ash.

\textbf{Corin} — the foundling whose name was written before a face. Veno has tried three times to buy his ear for the College or the Sere factors and has been refused three times, and has begun to suspect that the boy is not an account at all but a creditor, which is a category he has no word for.

\textbf{Avis Wake} — a Council member who votes against taxing the glass boats and whom Veno pays to do so, which is an arrangement the Council calls representation and Veno calls the cheapest insurance in Vhalen.

\CRsheetheading{Arc So Far}
No chapters are written yet. He enters in the Greybank counting house, smiling at a widow while declining to touch a slate, and the reader will take him for a merchant until the first damp document turns a household’s debt into nothing.

\CRsheetheading{Chapter Log}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.4em,itemsep=1pt,topsep=3pt,parsep=0pt]

\item No chapters written yet; first appearance planned for Ch 130 (the Greybank counting house, the offer for the boat-share).

\end{itemize}
}
